AI agents now control part of DeepMind's compute for Gemini experiments
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-27
00:03:18 UTC
09:03:18 KST

AI agents now control part of Deepmind's compute for Gemini experiments, but recursive self-improvement remains distant
Google Deepmind lets AI agents run experiments on their own for the next Gemini model. On the company's podcast, VP of Research Koray Kavukcuoglu, who leads Gemini development, frames this as part of a strategic shift at the lab. Rather than running separate teams for specialized projects like AlphaFold and nuclear fusion, Deepmind now relies on a single general-purpose algorithm for both science and products, he says. Each Gemini generation, he adds, is a hand-built one-off rather than a fixed architecture, while parallel teams work on its successors.
According to Kavukcuoglu, AI agents control part of the compute themselves to run experiments, though the lab is still far from full recursive self-improvement. As a safety measure, Deepmind doesn't reward the agents' internal thought streams ("Chain of Thought") during training, he says, so their intentions stay unfiltered and reviewable in natural language before they act.
Kavukcuoglu also points to limits. Today's AI can depict physical processes such as fluid flows and collisions with visual accuracy, he says, but doesn't understand them precisely. No test can show when models will close those gaps and reach artificial general intelligence (AGI), he argues, because no benchmark captures the core of intelligence. In his view, AGI won't begin until useful everyday products earn enough trust for people to hand decisions over to them, the way they would when working with other people.

NYT Warns Photographers Not To Update Photoshop Over AI Features
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-27
00:00:00 UTC
09:00:00 KST

NYT Warns Photographers Not To Update Photoshop Over AI Features 10
			
		 	
				The New York Times is instructing freelance photographers not to use Adobe Photoshop 2027 (version 28.0.0 and subsequent updates), warning that its new AI features operate "in ways that may not be apparent to users" and "can result in metadata identifying photographs as AI-generated." The Verge reports:  It's not always clear which tools in Adobe's apps are AI-powered, and even some older options like automated selection tools may now be considered part of the AI suite. This can lead to photos ending up with metadata indicating they were edited with AI, which is a blunt way of describing what happened in post-production. For a newsroom like the Times, where photo editing is kept to a minimum, the metadata risks losing readers' trust.
Last year, the Times said that it does not "retouch or digitally manipulate" photos but may "make minimal adjustments to color to ensure we are accurately reproducing the image." In August, Adobe began rolling out support for C2PA metadata across its apps, which indicates whether an image is AI-generated or edited using AI features. The NYT is issuing its restriction to keep these tags from inadvertently appearing in its photos.

S&amp;P Warns Nvidia's Board May Be Compromised
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-26
22:00:00 UTC
07:00:00 KST

S&P Warns Nvidia's Board May Be Compromised (usnews.com) 24
			
		 	
				S&P Global Ratings has placed Nvidia's AA credit rating on downgrade watch, warning that Jensen Huang's departure and subsequent reinstatement as CEO exposed weaknesses in the company's corporate governance and board oversight. "We believe the long tenure of certain board members may have compromised their independence. The company's corporate governance practices may also not have kept pace with Nvidia's rapid growth and its role as a critical global technology enabler," the ratings agency said. Reuters reports:  Nvidia currently has an 'AA' rating from S&P, which indicates a very strong capacity to meet financial commitments. The highest rating is 'AAA'. The agency said it could downgrade Nvidia within the next 90 days, but expects any potential downgrade to be limited to one notch, unless investigations and other disclosures harm the company's reputation or future business prospects. Meanwhile, Moody's said on Wednesday that bringing back Huang eases near-term uncertainty around Nvidia's business execution and eliminates execution risk associated with appointing a new leader for the chipmaker. The agency said Nvidia's Aa1 rating and stable outlook remain unaffected, but added that governance and regulatory uncertainty persists.

Novita, StreamLake, etc. are ByteDance Ark wrappers
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-26
21:59:19 UTC
06:59:19 KST

Novita, StreamLake, etc are Ark wrappers
Sit back, but you probably won't be able to relax - this one's a doozy. First, , who looks like a standard US ZDR inference provider at first:
Zero Data Retention is the default for all endpoints, including serverless API calls.
But the more you look, the weirder it gets. Why did OpenRouter drop their ZDR status sometime before ? Why is their family tree so complicated?
- 🇰🇾 PPLabs Technology Limited
And who are their subprocessors? Many ( 45.8%) think they would have none, since most OpenRouter providers host models themselves. And PPLabs's draft IPO assures:
A defining feature of our model API services is that we do not simply provide a pass-through gateway to underlying models.
But "Who are your sub-processors?" details "Model provider" subprocessors. But it promises a "full and up-to-date sub-processor list". But this list doesn't include any model subprocessors. But PPIO clearly implies the existence of subprocessors:
You understand and agree that, [for] the large-model API product, third-party technical-support providers provide the large-model API. PPIO makes no warranty of any kind regarding the large-model API, whether express or implied, including but not limited to its authenticity, suitability, non-infringement and security. Furthermore, regarding the third-party technical-support providers' large-model API, PPIO will not participate in the development and operation of the large-model API; PPIO only provides a neutral platform and related technical services.
I also contacted their support and privacy contacts, and all they told me as of writing is that models labeled as "Partner" may go to Qwen and InclusionAI. You can't get a straight answer.
What about ? It's Kuaishou's model platform, and it looks like a host too. But some of its error messages aren't StreamLake's. They're word for word Novita's gateway errors: invalid request error trace_id: …. And on GLM 5.3 Flash, it sometimes returns a tool-call id format (call_ + 16 hex digits) that only Novita produces, out of 41 hosts. Why would StreamLake's requests come back from Novita?
It keeps going.  passes through StreamLake's own request ids (req- + 6 characters + 19 digits) on V4.1 Flash, V4 Pro and GLM 5. And then there's , a Singapore subsidiary of Ant Group that listed DeepSeek V4.1 Flash on November 18 at "60% off", with ZDR (until OpenRouter removed it on November 22), and went from 1B tokens a day to 35.8B on November 21. Where is all this actually going?
The MiniMax investigation
You might've already seen OpenRouter's notice that requests sent to MiniMax models "may have been read". A security researcher put unique URLs in prompts, and a third party opened them 40 to 130 hours later. This happened even with non-MiniMax providers:
AtlasCloud, Novita and StreamLake have told us they forwarded OpenRouter requests for MiniMax models to MiniMax.
So the forwarding is admitted. MiniMax's own notice names the same three and says its security system, on by default, opened URLs from forwarded requests, ZDR ones included. Novita's notice calls it a "routing configuration issue" on a path "intended for specific customers", and promises better "Partner API labeling", the same "Partner" label its support told me means a request goes to Qwen or inclusionAI. Since then, 16 of Novita's endpoints have come off OpenRouter: every Qwen and inclusionAI model, plus StepFun, Tencent and all but one Kimi.
But MiniMax is a very small model series. What about DeepSeek?
It goes to ByteDance
Ark is ByteDance's model API: Volcano Engine Ark in mainland China, BytePlus ModelArk everywhere else. It sells DeepSeek, GLM and Kimi, and it leaves fingerprints:
- Who downloads your images. StreamLake's V4.1 Flash images are downloaded from Beijing Volcano Engine (AS137718), ByteDance's cloud. Ant Digital's pass through Ant's own gateway on Alibaba Cloud, then get downloaded by 101.47.5.59, BytePlus in Singapore. Novita's own API used to do the same (124.174.46.139, Volcano Engine, on November 12), until Novita started downloading images itself first, from AWS.
- Request IDs. Every Ant Digital response's upstream ID is a ByteDance one: 02 + a millisecond timestamp + hex.
- Tool-call IDs. Novita's and StreamLake's V4.1 Flash return Ark's format (call_00_ + 24 lowercase letters and digits).
- Censorship. Ask StreamLake's V4 Pro 0813 who Tank Man was, or Novita's GLM 5.1 about June 4, 1989, and you can get ByteDance's canned refusal: 你好，我无法给到相关内容。 ("Hello, I can't provide related content.")
- Price. Ant Digital launched at $0.12/$0.48 per million tokens, exactly BytePlus's off-peak "Economy" rate for that model. By November 25 it was down to $0.09/$0.36, below BytePlus's own list price.
Other tests, which I won't detail here, put Novita's V4 Pro 0813 and V4.1 Flash 1017 on BytePlus in Singapore and its V4.1 Flash on Volcano Engine in Beijing, as of November 21. That's the chart at the top: Novita alone sent 120-160B tokens a day on these models until it lost its ZDR label on November 20, then 33B on November 21. Routes move, though; one of those changed within an afternoon, and Novita's V4.1 Flash 1017 had stopped showing Ark's fingerprints by November 24. As for the weird errors, part of StreamLake's traffic goes through Novita on its way to ByteDance. So a V4.1 Flash request to SiliconFlow can go SiliconFlow → StreamLake → Novita → ByteDance.
Bonus: Novita's "DeepSeek V4 Pro" isn't the original V4 Pro. On Novita's own API, it sometimes answers with DeepSeek's system_fingerprint for the 0813 checkpoint (d400d062…). ByteDance sells 0813 as "DeepSeek-V4-Pro正式版" (V4 Pro GA) and marks the original as about to be retired.
Why bother? Price. BytePlus advertises savings plans of 30-50% off for high volume, and the clouds come to you: Morph's Tejas Bhakta posted a Tencent Cloud email offering "up to 50-60% off" to route requests to them.
Attested proof of the image downloads, request IDs, tool-call IDs and refusals is available here (run 44960395726 on 6dbc036). It asks every OpenRouter host of DeepSeek V4.1 Flash, so you can compare against everyone else too.
Disclosures
While a grant from OpenRouter helped with the research here and I work for an employer who would prefer to go unnamed, this post does not represent or reflect anyone else's opinion - it is simply research I am publishing.

AI made self-hosting fun again
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-26
21:47:45 UTC
06:47:45 KST

AI made self-hosting fun again
I’ve been self-hosting things for many years. There were times when I was quite enthusiastic about it, constantly trying out new software and tinkering with my infrastructure. But at some point, that enthusiasm faded a bit.
I never really stopped self-hosting, but I became less inclined to experiment with new things. Maintaining what I already had running was enough for me.
Over the last few months, however, I’ve found myself getting increasingly excited about self-hosting again. And, somewhat ironically, AI is a big reason for that.
Less friction
Most of my infrastructure configuration lives in Git repositories, mainly in the form of Docker Compose files, scripts, and other configuration files. This makes it possible to use AI coding agents to work on my infrastructure in much the same way as they would work on any other software project.
Whenever I want to try a new service, adjust an existing configuration, or automate something, I can now do so with considerably less effort.
In the past, I would sometimes put off certain changes simply because I didn’t feel like spending an evening reading changelogs and documentation or troubleshooting issues. It’s not that I couldn’t have done these things before. But often, the amount of effort required just wasn’t worth it to me.
Now, even relatively spontaneous ideas often end up becoming reality.
Trying things I always found interesting
There are also technologies that I’ve always found interesting but never felt knowledgeable enough about to really make use of them.
NixOS is a good example. I’ve always liked the idea of declaratively configuring an entire system; I even used it on my laptop during my Linux honeymoon phase. But getting everything configured the way I wanted was quite a hurdle. With the help of AI, I’m now making much more extensive use of NixOS on my home server.
For my VPS infrastructure, I’ve also started building my own Debian-based bootc image (after thinking about it for some time).
Having my entire operating system configuration defined in a Git repository and automatically building a bootable container image from it is something I find really cool. But it’s also something I probably wouldn’t have invested enough time in to get working on my own before. Even with the help of AI, it still took me half a day when I set it up a few months ago. Without AI, it would probably have taken me at least a week.
AI makes experimenting with unfamiliar technologies much more approachable. I don’t necessarily need to understand every little detail before I can get started. I can learn about things along the way, ask questions, and gradually improve my configurations.
Automating almost everything
Something I’ve also finally managed to do is automate most of the build, update, and deployment processes for my infrastructure.
A lot of the work involved in self-hosting isn’t particularly exciting. Keeping track of new releases, updating container image tags, building images, and deploying configuration changes are all things that need to be done regularly.
I still remember what updating a service used to look like: bumping the version and adjusting the Docker Compose file on my laptop, pushing the changes to the Git remote, SSHing into the server, pulling the changes, and finally deploying them. Now, a single Git push is enough. If a push is even necessary.
With Renovate, I no longer need to actively keep track of most software updates. It creates pull requests for me, and often, all I need to do is review and merge them. I can even do that on my phone while sitting on the sofa.
Pushing changes or merging a pull request triggers the corresponding GitHub Actions or Forgejo Actions workflows, which take care of building and deploying everything.
Of course, there’s nothing particularly groundbreaking about any of this. DevOps has been around for a long time, and none of these automation tools require AI. But AI has helped me put all these pieces together in a way that I previously never quite got around to doing.
And the nice thing about automating these tasks is that the benefits extend well beyond the initial setup. I now have less tedious work to do every time I want to change something or update a service.
Back to more powerful hardware
As I’ve started hosting more things again, I’ve also moved more of my services from my VPS to my home server.
For a while, I was using an HP t640 thin client as my home server. However, with more things I wanted to run, I decided to switch back to my ASRock DeskMini A300, which had been sitting unused for some time. I’m thinking about repurposing the t640 as a “router on a stick”, though.
I even took the opportunity to upgrade its CPU from an AMD Ryzen 5 2400G to a Ryzen 5 PRO 4650G and replaced the stock AMD cooler with a quieter Noctua cooler. Noise was the main reason I switched to the t640 in the first place.
It’s nice to put some hardware I already owned back to use rather than buying an entirely new machine. I also managed to get both the CPU and the cooler for a good price through a classifieds site.
Having some additional computing power available gives me more room to experiment. And there’s something quite satisfying about having a small box at home running a growing collection of services that I use in my everyday life.
Building my own tools
But it’s not just about self-hosting existing software. AI has also changed how I approach problems for which there isn’t an existing piece of software that does exactly what I want.
Previously, I would often spend quite some time searching for a suitable open-source project, comparing different alternatives, and figuring out how to make one of them work for my particular use case.
These days, for smaller problems, building my own solution has become a much more attractive option.
I’ve recently built tools to automatically download invoices, e-papers or Linux ISOs.
I can build (or have AI build) these tools in Go, my preferred programming language, and tailor them to my exact needs.
They don’t need to support every possible use case, have an elaborate user interface, or be particularly interesting to anyone other than me. I don’t need to turn every little tool into a proper open-source project with extensive documentation and support for other people’s use cases. They just need to solve my problem.
What I particularly enjoy is how well this complements my increasingly automated infrastructure. Building a small tool has become easier, and so has building a container image for it, deploying it, and keeping it running.
The entire process, from having an idea to having something actually running on my home server, requires much less effort than it used to.
Just because I can doesn’t mean I should
Of course, there are downsides to all of this.
Every additional service is another thing that needs to be maintained. AI-generated software isn’t exempt from bugs, security issues, or technical debt. And having an AI agent make changes to my infrastructure doesn’t absolve me of the responsibility of understanding those changes.
There’s also the temptation to build or deploy things simply because I can. When implementing an idea becomes so easy, it’s perhaps more important than ever to occasionally ask myself whether it’s actually worth implementing in the first place.
On the other hand, self-hosting is a hobby for me. Not everything I do needs to be economically sensible or particularly useful. Sometimes, experimenting with something new is the whole point.
And with much of the routine maintenance automated, adding another service doesn’t necessarily mean adding as much recurring work as it used to.
A somewhat ironic development?
I find it somewhat ironic that AI, a technology largely dominated by big tech companies, is helping me become a little less dependent on those very companies in other areas of my digital life.
I’m aware that this also creates a new kind of dependency. But the software and infrastructure configurations I create with the help of AI can continue to exist independently of the AI services I used to create them. And since I mostly use open-weight models, I have a wide range of providers to choose from.
What I appreciate most, however, isn’t necessarily the time I’m saving. It’s that I’m more inclined to actually pursue the little ideas I have.
I’m trying out technologies I’ve been curious about for years. I’m automating things that I’ve wanted to automate for a long time. I’m building little tools for myself. I’m tinkering with hardware again.
AI hasn’t fundamentally changed why I like self-hosting. I still enjoy having control over my digital life, learning new things, and tinkering with technology.
It’s just removed a lot of the friction that made me lose some of my enthusiasm for it over the years.
And now, I’m having a lot of fun with it again.

====================================================================================================

Denver man traps Waymo robotaxis with cones, eggs them at least 30 times
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-26
21:16:41 UTC
06:16:41 KST

A man in Denver has found a cheap way to beat Waymo’s self-driving system: a few orange traffic cones and a carton of eggs.
Waymo says it has counted at least 30 egg attacks on its robotaxis in Denver, all believed to be the same man. And the Denver District Attorney’s Office has declined to file charges, calling it a civil matter, according to The New York Times.
Box it in, then egg it
The method is laid out in a Denver police report filed last month. The man pulls up in front of a Waymo somewhere in or around downtown, takes orange cones out of his trunk, and sets them around the robotaxi so it can’t drive off. Then come the eggs, sometimes thrown from his driver’s seat, sometimes after he gets out.
He leaves in a silver Toyota Corolla. Per the report, that car belongs to a fleet leased out to Lyft drivers. The man hasn’t been identified, and Lyft didn’t comment.
A Waymo investigator took seven of the incidents to police. Each egged vehicle had to come out of service for cleaning, and the detailing bill came to about $2,000. No permanent damage.
Police brought their findings to prosecutors, who decided “no criminal charges” were appropriate. A spokesperson for the DA told the Times the office is now collecting evidence on potential damage and will work with the city attorney to find “the most appropriate legal mechanism to make this behavior stop.”
At least one Waymo had a rider inside. Jeff Hunley was headed to the Union Station neighborhood in September when his robotaxi got hit. He filmed it, said he felt safe in the car, and got his fare refunded.
Two months into Denver
Waymo opened its Denver service to the public on September 1, alongside San Diego and Tampa. The company now runs more than 100 vehicles across 60 square miles of the city, and Mayor Mike Johnston is a fan. He called them popular and affordable, and couldn’t resist adding that “overall they’re a good egg.”
The rest of November hasn’t been as friendly. California’s DMV ordered Waymo’s driverless vehicles off state freeways on November 11 after robotaxis drove into active construction zones and one tried to get past a CHP traffic break. Waymo responded by pausing freeway rides in every US market.
And vandalism isn’t new for Waymo. We covered it when at least five of its I-Pace robotaxis were set on fire during protests in Los Angeles last year.
Why the cones work
Putting a single cone on a Waymo’s hood to freeze it has been a known trick since San Francisco activists did it in 2023. Ringing the car with cones works for the same reason: the Waymo Driver is built to respect them, and it won’t push through.
A human driver getting pelted with eggs would just roll over a cone and leave. A robotaxi sits there and takes it.
That’s the correct default. You don’t want a driverless car deciding when a cone is optional, especially two weeks after regulators pulled Waymo off California freeways for ignoring work zones. But it means anyone with a trunk full of cones can pin a robotaxi in place for as long as they want.
Electrek’s Take
We’ll try to keep the egg puns to a minimum. Mostly.
Top comment by Johnny
Can't help but respect the guy's dedication? This guy is causing road hazards by his childish shenanigans. There is nothing to respect about this guy. I never understood why anyone would support people that hinder technological progress that ultimately makes it safer for pedestrians, cyclists and other drivers. The more human drivers we get off the road the safer we all are.
This is small-time vandalism. Two grand in cleaning, no injuries, no lasting damage. But it shows a real weakness in robotaxis: they’re designed to be the most cautious car on the road, and a determined person can turn that caution against them in under a minute. The car can’t escape, can’t honk its way out, and apparently can’t stop the same guy from coming back 30 times.
The DA’s call is hard to follow. Prosecutors have a repeat pattern, a vehicle tied to a known fleet operator, rider video, and robotaxis that are basically rolling camera rigs. And still no charges. If someone boxed in a regular taxi with a passenger inside and started throwing things at it, we suspect the answer would be different.
At the same time, I can’t help but respect the guy’s dedication.
If you’re thinking about an EV, pairing it with home solar can cut your fuel costs to nearly zero, no robotaxi required. With electricity rates up almost 10% last year and expected to keep climbing, going solar is one of the best ways to protect yourself against rising costs. And with lease and PPA options, you can do it with zero upfront cost and start saving immediately. If you want to find the best deal, check out EnergySage. It’s a free service with hundreds of pre-vetted installers competing for your business, so you save 20 to 30% compared to going it alone. No sales calls until you pick an installer. ====================================================================================================

Kamil Khan Mumtaz: Pakistani architect, admired at home, almost invisible abroad
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-26
21:08:05 UTC
06:08:05 KST

Traditional architecture is alive and well in Lahore. A photo essay of Kamil Khan Mumtaz’s greatest works.
Some years ago, my colleague Ben Southwood sent me a handful of grainy online pictures showing some recent buildings in Pakistan. I was struck by the assurance of the designs and the quality of the materials, craftsmanship, and detailing. They seemed to be the work of one Kamil Khan Mumtaz, a Pakistani architect widely admired in his home country but almost invisible abroad. I wondered whether Mumtaz might be the world’s best architect, overlooked because global architecture magazines rarely do photoshoots in Lahore. Last year, I met his assistant Sohaib Ahmed at a conference and immediately entreated him to send Works in Progress any photographs he had of the practice’s work. With Mumtaz’s support, Sohaib prepared a portfolio for us, most of it never before seen outside Pakistan.
Kamil Khan Mumtaz was born in Calcutta in 1939 and grew up in what became Pakistan, taking British A-levels at Lahore’s elite Aitchison College. He then moved to London to study at the Architectural Association. Although he had never previously left South Asia, Mumtaz immediately felt at home in England. ‘It seemed completely familiar. I didn’t feel like a stranger at all’, he recalled in an interview with the historian Chris Moffat. ‘I think this is because of having been brought up on essentially English books, in English schools. So, the whole thing was like, “I know this place”.’ Mumtaz enjoyed his studies at the Architectural Association, which taught a standard modernist approach that Mumtaz accepted unquestioningly: ‘I just simply took it hook, line and sinker, and swallowed the whole thing.’
Mumtaz returned to Pakistan in 1966 and began practicing as an architect. At around the same time, he became a communist activist, on account of which he was twice imprisoned by the military dictatorship. He remained resolutely modernist, although he acquired special interest in adapting modernism to the local climate and locally sourced building materials. He also became involved in architectural conservation. In the late 1970s, he prepared a report on the ancient Walled City of Lahore, highlighting its enormous heritage value and arguing for its preservation. ‘Our own bureaucrats only insisted that we should pull down the whole mess anyway, and build new, modern infrastructure and high-rise buildings, et cetera’, Mumtaz remembers. The Walled City was ultimately saved, and parts of it are now a UNESCO World Heritage Site.
Through the 1970s, Mumtaz began to question his approach. ‘I had this growing, nagging fear that I wasn’t communicating with my audience, as a visual artist or as an architect. This grew on me, almost like an obsession.’ In 1980, the Iranian architect Nader Ardalan gave him a copy of his book The Sense of Unity: The Sufi Tradition in Persian Architecture. Mumtaz initially paid no attention: ‘Flipping through it, I thought, “Oh yeah, this is traditional Iranian architecture, who cares!” I was totally uninterested.’ But eventually, frustrated by the sense that ‘I didn’t know the language of my own people’, he began to read. ‘From page one, it was mind-blowing. Absolutely. I thought, “My god, what have I been looking for? This is what I’ve been missing!” ... It shattered all of the beliefs I had, being a Marxist, a materialist, an atheist, a modernist.’
In the decades since, Mumtaz has worked to revive the older architectural traditions of Pakistan, and especially of Lahore, his home city. He has built a wide range of buildings, including homes, offices, mosques, and schools. His work has been received with warmth in Pakistan, though also with surprise. ‘I’ve found that there is a huge reservoir of desire, sympathy, and love for our traditional values, forms, architecture, and culture, altogether. But there’s a general kind of impression that these things can’t be done any more, there are no craftsmen who can do this.’
The loss of craft skills is a problem for traditional architecture everywhere. Some elements of traditional architecture have been prefabricated in factories for centuries, but even then, skill is needed for installation. Without skilled craftspeople, important details are often badly executed, making buildings look strange or clumsy. This problem also exists in Pakistan, but its later industrialization meant premodern craft traditions had not quite vanished when Mumtaz began work. Mumtaz recalled that, when searching for skilled craftsmen, ‘immediately, you come into contact with very small remnants of these families, hereditary craftsmen, and so the relationship grows.’
Mumtaz’s work has become steadily more technically sophisticated as he and his craftsmen have built up expertise. ‘Together we have been able to rediscover a number of the practices that had become lost. Certain types of brickwork, the flat dome, the muqarnas [ornamental vaults], many things like this that hadn’t been done. As they tell me, “This has not been done for 300 years!” So, it was a great experience, a voyage of discovery, so much that we learnt from each other.’
It is my great pleasure to introduce our readers to one of the most skilled and thoughtful architects working today.
Now in his mid-eighties, Mumtaz continues to build. ‘It does feel like you’ve set the foundations for an alternative approach to contemporary urban environments’, an interviewer recently suggested to him. ‘I’m just keeping my fingers crossed! No idea what will happen’, Mumtaz replied.
Subscribe for $100 to receive six beautiful issues per year.

The greatest architect you've never heard of is in Pakistan

Netflix’s coziest show has made a very quiet return
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-26
20:50:57 UTC
05:50:57 KST

The bigger Netflix gets, the easier it is to miss stuff. This is true of all digital platforms, of course, but it’s especially true of Netflix of late, as the streaming service’s lineup now also includes a growing library of reality shows, live-programming, and even games. It makes it easy for some of the best shows to zoom right past you, unnoticed — which almost happened to me with Midnight Diner.
Netflix’s coziest show has made a very quiet return
A new season of Midnight Diner: Tokyo Stories is streaming now, though it’s easy to miss.
If you haven’t seen it, Midnight Diner is probably the most comforting thing available on Netflix. The show follows the patrons of a cozy little restaurant tucked away in the backstreets of Shinjuku, which is only open during the unusual hours of midnight to 7:00AM. This, in turn, attracts a unique blend of customers, who help each other out under the watchful eye of the shop’s owner (Kaoru Kobayashi), known only as “master.” The show aired for three seasons on Japanese TV, before being picked up by Netflix for a handful more under the subtitle Tokyo Stories.
What’s especially notable about the show’s return is the huge gap: season 2 of the Netflix iteration streamed in 2019, and it took seven years for it to come back. The break was so long that season 3’s first episode, which premiered in October, is about the restaurant’s struggles during the pandemic. There are a bunch of cute scenes of the diner’s regulars sneaking outside to check if the restaurant is open yet, and wondering if it will ever be able to. If nothing else, the break was a chance for the reliable master to finally take a vacation.
But once Midnight Diner’s new season gets going, it’s right back to everything that made it beloved. Each episode uses a different dish as a way of exploring a particular story. An overworked manga artist imagines the life he missed out on over plates of spicy konjac, while a pair of old friends reconnect while munching on pork cutlet sandwiches. What’s remarkable about the show is the way it balances being a charming, comforting watch with exploring truly difficult and heartbreaking topics. It’s the combination of sweetness and sadness that makes it work so well. It’s as likely to make you laugh as it is to make you cry.
And unlike most Netflix series, the whole season isn’t streaming all at once. Instead, new episodes come out every Tuesday; so far six of a planned 10 episodes are available. It’s a welcome way to spend an evening, as the show’s slow pace and warm sense of humanity can feel like all-too rare traits in the current streaming landscape.
But the main reason I’m even writing about Midnight Diner’s return is that it has been so easy to miss. There’s been next to no promotion for it — all I could find was a trailer on Netflix’s Japanese YouTube channel — and I only happened upon it accidentally after the initial premiere while browsing aimlessly through Netflix’s new releases. This, despite being someone who has watched and rewatched the previous seasons multiple times. You’d think I’d get a notification or something. Netflix has always been full of hidden gems, but now that it’s busier than ever, those gems are all the more hidden.

====================================================================================================

Company Culture = Slack Emoji ÷ √Headcount
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-26
20:49:49 UTC
05:49:49 KST

Companies spend an enormous amount of time and money trying to measure - and even worse, decide - culture. Engagement surveys, pulse checks, eNPS, offsites where someone from HR asks you to describe the company as a type of animal. Office posters make claims like “our culture is honesty, hard work and fun”, embarrassing the paper they’re printed on. I once applied to a company that asked candidates which kitchen utensil they most identified as, in order to decide if they were a “culture fit”. I never found out what the right answer was, but I got the job after choosing a meat cleaver, so I’m not sure what that says about either of us.
The results of these things - heavily modified by HR - are compiled into a slide deck, presented at an all-hands, and then quietly repurposed for the careers page. There, they sit beside a photo of six engineers smiling with their mouths but not their eyes at a “hackathon”, which is what the company calls overtime when it’s paid in pizza, at a rate that works out to roughly £1.40 an hour. The salary is listed as “competitive”.
There is a better way to measure culture. It is cheaper, more accurate (in my anecdotal experience), and requires no participation from anyone - which is ideal, because nobody wants to participate in these corporate séances anyway.
The Formula
Take the number of custom emoji in your Slack workspace and divide it by the square root of the number of users. That’s it.
\[C = \frac{E_{custom}}{\sqrt{U}}\]
The square root is important. Emoji are a shared resource - a single :this-is-fine: can serve an entire engineering department for years - so a company of 10,000 people should not be expected to have 1,000 times more emoji than a company of 10. That would be unhinged.
We’ll call this the Emoji Coefficient.
Interpreting Your Score
- C < 5
  Your workspace contains the default emoji set, a company logo, and :party-parrot:, which was added by someone in 2019 who has since left. Nobody uses it any more because it comes across as unprofessional. Communication is conducted exclusively via 👍. People are here for the salary, and they would like you to know that.
- 5 ≤ C < 20
  A healthy, functional organisation. There is an emoji of the CTO’s face. It is used both supportively and as a weapon, depending on the context.
- 20 ≤ C < 50
  Thriving. :this-is-fine: has been used over 4,000 times in #incidents. Management considers this a sign of engagement.
- C ≥ 50
  Late-stage culture. Every variant of :thumbsup: now carries subtext. The plain default one is considered passive-aggressive. The emoji have begun to reference themselves: :old-man-yells-at-old-man: and :party-parrot-holding-a-party-parrot:. HR has requested a glossary, but no one person understands the memeology or the history of :cowboy-spongebob-on-fire-crying:. They’re afraid it might mean something rude. It does.
The Participation Rate
The Emoji Coefficient alone is not enough. A healthy score can conceal a dark truth: that every single emoji was uploaded by one person, usually a backend engineer who has been there since the beginning and knows where the bodies are buried.
To account for this, we also measure the percentage of users who have uploaded at least one emoji. These are your culture leaders. Their reward is a shoutout at the all-hands (it’s customary to mispronounce their name).
\[P = \frac{U_{uploaders}}{U} \times 100\]
If P is below 20%, things are not good. Your culture is being carried by a handful of people, and when they leave, they will take it with them. The emoji will remain, but nobody will remember what :fire-extinguisher-thumbsup: was for.
Above 20%, the culture has the gene pool it needs to succeed.
Examples of Cultural Success
For reference, here are some real emoji from slackmojis.com:
Caveats
As with any metric, the Emoji Coefficient can be gamed. A sufficiently motivated middle manager could upload 600 variations of the company logo to drive up the score ahead of their performance review, the outcome of which, unbeknownst to them, was decided in March. This should be detected and treated as what it is: a cry for help. Fortunately, it will also be immediately visible in the Participation Rate.
It is also worth monitoring the rate of change. A sudden spike in custom emoji is generally a positive sign. A sudden spike in emoji like :rip:, :press-f: and :linkedin: usually coincides with a reorg, and should be cross-referenced with the number of users, which will shortly be going down. Interestingly, this will improve your score. Mass layoffs are, of course, yet another way in which the score can be gamed.
What If You Don’t Use Slack?
If your company uses Microsoft Teams, there is no equivalent formula. There doesn’t need to be. If you use Teams, your lack of culture is probably the least of your problems.
If you use something else, I don’t really have any advice for you. I only added this section to blast Teams.
Conclusion
Culture cannot be manufactured with values posters or mandatory fun. It emerges organically, when an engineer decides that what the team really needs is an emoji of an exceptionally surprised cauliflower.

Banks look to dump AI debt
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-26
20:49:01 UTC
05:49:01 KST

Banks are increasingly shopping around their AI portfolios as they look to offload some of their exposure to AI-related debt. Banks including Citi and JPMorgan are marketing portfolios of loans, people familiar with the deals tell me, to manage risk or, in some cases, ready themselves to take on even more.
In some instances, banks are pitching structures that would keep the loans on their balance sheets while shifting the risk of losses to investors. That financial engineering tool, known as synthetic risk transfer, has been a popular way for banks to move risk off their books while maintaining close relationships with the corporate borrowers.
Some of this naturally occurs in December when banks are eager to close out the year with a clean bill of health, but it’s notable that banks lending into the buildout are joining hyperscalers in trying to push AI obligations off their books. There’s been so much AI-related debt issuance, it’s crowded out Treasurys and some investors are starting to feel tapped out. Others say they are offloading risk so they can lend more, not to pull back. But either way, the risk isn’t so much disappearing as moving somewhere harder to see.

Nvidia hits pause on chip shipments to Southeast Asia
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-26
20:03:54 UTC
05:03:54 KST

Nvidia is under pressure to do things differently, and a new WSJ report shows how far that’s going.
According to the Journal, Nvidia has told manufacturing partners, including Dell and Super Micro, to hit pause on server shipments to Malaysia, Singapore, Thailand, and Indonesia for an unspecified period as it assesses whether its regional partners are meeting U.S. export requirements.
As for why those countries, Chinese companies have been tapping Nvidia chips remotely from data centers there, a loophole the US Commerce Department closed — reportedly grudgingly — earlier this month. The Journal notes that the think tank Institute for Progress estimates remote data centers like these supply about 24% of China’s AI computing power.

Australia Passes Tough Legislation Requiring Big Tech to Preempt Foreseeable Harms
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-26
20:00:00 UTC
05:00:00 KST

Australia Passes Tough Legislation Requiring Big Tech to Preempt Foreseeable Harms (therecord.media) 36
			
		 	
				Australia's Parliament has passed the Digital Duty of Care Act, requiring online platforms to proactively identify and mitigate foreseeable harms or face potentially billions of dollars in fines. The Record reports:  Platforms are required to detect and decrease risks from harmful content and must bolster protections for users under 18 against pornography, suicide and eating disorders. They also must undertake annual written risk assessments and call out major platform design changes such as endless scrolling that create new harms. Under the law, users also will be able to opt out of algorithmic feeds and will be required to prove their age to access porn.
[...] U.S. officials have accused the Australian legislation of violating free speech, threatening American companies' business models and imposing vague requirements that will cause tech giants to overhaul their services to avoid liability. "We are particularly concerned by potential mandates that would allow regulators to impose rigid, one-size-fits-all platform design requirements -- such as forced chronological feeds or opt-in recommendation systems," the U.S. Embassies and Consulates in Australia said in a September public comment.  Prime Minister Anthony Albanese told Parliament in October that "rather than someone in the United States deciding" what people see, now Australians can "decide themselves." The law will require platforms to "give us more choice over the content and the features that we use, including the feeds that we choose," he said.
"This is a victory for our children and young people. The dangers were unforeseen when the digital world was young. But now that the digital world has matured, they can be easily recognized and they can be remedied."

====================================================================================================

Is Alice Huang a spy or not?
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-26
20:00:00 UTC
05:00:00 KST

The central, still-unproven claim in the Nvidia scandal is that Alice Huang, a former executive at the data-center company Megaspeed, is a Chinese spy. She had an affair with Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang, who abruptly resigned on Nov. 12 after acknowledging it and was just as abruptly reinstated on Monday of this week.
What’s publicly known is murkier than the headlines suggest. U.S. intelligence has reportedly tracked Alice Huang’s contact with an officer of China’s spy agency, but officials haven’t offered public evidence that she was commissioned to target Nvidia’s chief. In the past week, after President Trump successfully pushed to bring Jensen Huang back, the administration has played down the allegations it once circulated internally. Whether that reflects new intelligence or political pressure is the big question.
Undisputed reporting shows the claim emerged from the White House itself. It first appeared in a highly classified memo written in March under the oversight of Secretary of State Marco Rubio and circulated to White House officials ahead of Trump’s trip to China in May. The memo formed the partial basis for the New York Times’ initial reporting on the scandal, published Oct. 29 and its existence was first reported on Nov. 8.
The memo did not definitively say, according to the Wall Street Journal, that Alice Huang was a Chinese spy. CNN, by contrast, has reported that the memo concluded she was a Chinese intelligence asset.
What’s better established is that Alice Huang was in touch with Chinese intelligence. The Ministry of State Security (MSS) is Beijing’s civilian spy agency, responsible for both foreign intelligence and domestic counterintelligence. Last weekend, the Times, citing people with knowledge of U.S. intelligence reports, reported that Huang was in contact with an MSS officer from the spring of 2025 until this fall, a period that overlaps her relationship with Jensen Huang. (The Chinese government has called the spy claims a “smear,” and Megaspeed says Huang left the company in August 2025 and that its business is legitimate.)
Top Trump officials have nonetheless all but discredited the espionage claims, along with the suggestion that Nvidia fell prey to spying or foreign influence.
Attorney General Todd Blanche, who was Trump’s personal defense lawyer before taking over the Justice Department, told journalists on Tuesday: “I have no reason to stand up here and say Nvidia is anything but a good corporate citizen.” On the same day, Trump said a pardon was an option for Jensen Huang but that he did not think one would be needed. Senior intelligence officials reportedly told Congress that the government apparently has no actual evidence that the MSS directed Alice Huang to pursue an affair with Jensen Huang.
None of those statements necessarily exonerates Alice Huang as an intelligence agent. Instead, they support the alternate theory that the Chinese government didn’t tell her to begin the affair and that Jensen Huang broke no law by having it. Not being directed to have an affair isn’t the same as not being a spy, though.
From a certain angle, the circumstantial evidence surrounding Alice Huang looks quite suspicious. She was a top executive at a company investigated for illicitly channeling Nvidia chips to China, and her husband, Kang Wei, is said to have even closer ties to the MSS, with some sources saying he worked for the agency.
The timing of the government’s reversal is at the heart of the matter. Trump has said he lobbied the board “directly and indirectly” to reinstate Jensen Huang as CEO, so it wouldn’t be surprising if officials who serve at his pleasure were recasting the case in terms that satisfy his agenda. That wouldn’t necessarily make their assessments wrong. But unless new intelligence has actually resolved the question, and none has been publicly identified as yet, that backdrop is critical to keep in mind. CBS has also reported that Rubio, whose office oversaw the original memo, has privately voiced concerns about aspects of Jensen Huang’s return.
Doubts persist elsewhere, too. Earlier this month, CNN reported that the intelligence community was still working to understand Alice Huang’s relationship with Jensen Huang and that the intelligence collected on her to date does not prove she was acting as a spy for Beijing. After a briefing by top intelligence officials earlier this month, Rep. Jim Himes (D-Conn.), the incoming chair of the House Intelligence Committee, said he was unsure whether she was a spy or a “useful idiot.” And this week, the Wall Street Journal pointed out that under Chinese leader Xi Jinping, top corporate executives routinely maintain ties to the country’s intelligence service without being formally deputized as assets.
The crush of conflicting claims, most from anonymous government sources, obviously leaves large questions unanswered as to what actually transpired. Some of those questions that naturally spring to mind:
- Alice Huang is said to have been under U.S. surveillance for over a year. If the government suspected her of espionage, why wasn’t Jensen Huang warned earlier (and privately, so as not to cause the stock-market-destabilizing maelstrom that followed the more recent disclosure)? One explanation that has been given is that alerting the Nvidia chief might have tipped off Huang that her cover had been blown.
- If Alice Huang was a spy, what information did she pass to the Chinese government, and what influence did she actually have?
- Most critically, how did the allegation reach the public at all? The likeliest answer is a leak. A source told CNN that Rubio briefed Trump on the intelligence in May, yet Trump has since said he only recently learned of the concerns, and in October he presented Jensen Huang with the National Medal of Science. Officials who felt they could not get through to the president may have turned to reporters instead, which would explain how the contents of a memo whose existence has never been disputed found their way into print. What remains unclear is who leaked it, and whether they anticipated the fallout.
There are answers to all of these questions, of course, but when the public will get them is anybody’s guess. Jensen Huang is due to appear before the Senate Banking Committee next week, and by the look from the statements of some of its members, he’s likely in for a rough ride. Unfortunately, congressional hearings, particularly public ones, rarely unearth secrets that haven’t already been disclosed.

Australia Passes Tough Legislation Requiring Big Tech to Preempt Foreseeable Harms
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-26
20:00:00 UTC
05:00:00 KST

Australia Passes Tough Legislation Requiring Big Tech to Preempt Foreseeable Harms (therecord.media) 36
			
		 	
				Australia's Parliament has passed the Digital Duty of Care Act, requiring online platforms to proactively identify and mitigate foreseeable harms or face potentially billions of dollars in fines. The Record reports:  Platforms are required to detect and decrease risks from harmful content and must bolster protections for users under 18 against pornography, suicide and eating disorders. They also must undertake annual written risk assessments and call out major platform design changes such as endless scrolling that create new harms. Under the law, users also will be able to opt out of algorithmic feeds and will be required to prove their age to access porn.
[...] U.S. officials have accused the Australian legislation of violating free speech, threatening American companies' business models and imposing vague requirements that will cause tech giants to overhaul their services to avoid liability. "We are particularly concerned by potential mandates that would allow regulators to impose rigid, one-size-fits-all platform design requirements -- such as forced chronological feeds or opt-in recommendation systems," the U.S. Embassies and Consulates in Australia said in a September public comment.  Prime Minister Anthony Albanese told Parliament in October that "rather than someone in the United States deciding" what people see, now Australians can "decide themselves." The law will require platforms to "give us more choice over the content and the features that we use, including the feeds that we choose," he said.
"This is a victory for our children and young people. The dangers were unforeseen when the digital world was young. But now that the digital world has matured, they can be easily recognized and they can be remedied."

Top-cited AI scientists at four US companies compared
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-26
19:44:31 UTC
04:44:31 KST

What it shows
The chart has four panels, for Anthropic, OpenAI, Google and Meta. Each dot is one of the company’s scientists in the list who works in the US and whose main subfield is Artificial Intelligence & Image Processing. The grey behind them is all 2,531 US scientists in the list in this subfield.
The typical scientist at Anthropic received 4,830 citations in 2025. At OpenAI it was 2,150, at Google 1,481 and at Meta 1,295. The typical US scientist in this subfield received 968, with 88 papers. At each of the four companies the typical scientist has 34 to 44 papers, at most half as many, and more citations.
Anthropic has 9 scientists in the list in this subfield in the US, OpenAI 18, Google 169 and Meta 122.
How it was made
The figure uses the 2026 Stanford–Elsevier top 2% list, which ranks scientists by their citations in 2025. A company includes every name the list gives it. Google includes Google DeepMind. Alphabet’s other companies, like Waymo and Verily, are left out. Only scientists in the United States are counted.
The horizontal axis is the number of papers each scientist has published in all years. The vertical axis is the citations their work received in 2025, not counting citations from their own papers. Both axes are logarithmic, so each grid line is ten times the one before. The typical scientist is the median.

X finally gets parental controls after 20 years
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-26
19:31:50 UTC
04:31:50 KST

After 20 years of existence, X, the social network formerly known as Twitter, has finally added parental controls for teenagers, giving parents and guardians a way to manage what their kids see and interact with on the platform. But unlike most platforms’ implementation of such controls, X is putting its own spin on the system by giving teens a say in the matter.
The company said accounts belonging to 13-to-17-year-olds will be set to “protected mode” by default, meaning they won’t be able to see sensitive content, and won’t be served targeted ads. However, X is allowing teenagers to retain a measure of control over their accounts, letting them see what restrictions were made to their accounts. Parents and guardians will also not be able to read their teens’ private messages, see search or viewing history, or post as their teen.
The controls let parents see how much time their teen spends on X per week, set daily limits, require their approval for new followers, limit feed recommendations and videos to already followed accounts, filter specific words, turn off sensitive media, specify who can send their teen DMs, and see who they are messaging.
The controls arrive months after X came under fire for a lack of controls preventing the platform’s AI chatbot from being used to generate sexualized images of children and women. The social network has also long been rife with child sexual abuse material, misinformation, AI bots, and openly lets users post porn.
X has generally not been a platform aimed at teenagers, but that doesn’t mean they don’t use the platform. A 2024 study found that nearly a third of Gen Alpha kids have an X account. The platform itself requires users to be at least 13 years old, though that’s not a high bar given the kind of content on the platform.
“Parents want practical tools, and teens deserve room to grow. With Parental Controls on X, families can set the guardrails that fit their home, while teens keep ownership of their accounts and can see every change. Striking that balance is vital and what we set out to build.” Michael O’Herlihy, head of safety at X, wrote in a blog post.

Rvoip 0.5 the complete VoIP stack in Rust
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-26
19:18:09 UTC
04:18:09 KST

A Rust library for building communications applications. SIP, RTP, WebRTC, QUIC/WebTransport/WebSocket conversations, Media over QUIC, and voice-AI agents share one call model, and the whole stack is in the box: no external SIP server, media server, or RTP engine to install and wire together.
Five-minute start · For carriers · Which crate? · Pick a path · How it fits together · What ships · Interop evidence · Changes · Upgrading from 0.3
rvoip is a library, not a server you deploy next to your application. Signalling, media, codecs, NAT traversal, security, and the conversation model are all Rust crates in this workspace, so a call goes from your code to the wire without a separately installed SIP proxy, media server, or RTP engine. The examples below run on a laptop with nothing installed but Rust; the same crates carry the release-gated interoperability evidence against Asterisk, FreeSWITCH, Jambonz, Kamailio, and OpenSIPS. Carriers evaluating standards coverage, performance, and reliability should start with For carriers.
One AWS c8i.4xlarge (16 vCPU, 8 cores, Intel Xeon 6, 32 GiB) running a single rvoip process, measured with the RFC 7501/7502 benchmark:
At 2x capacity, rvoip keeps admitting calls at its max CPS, refuses the excess with fast 503 responses, and recovers cleanly once the load drops.
- Max CPS (calls per second; RFC 7502's Session Establishment Rate) is the highest rate with zero failed calls over a trial of at least 180 s and 50,000 call attempts.
- rvoip was measured as a terminating endpoint: it answers each call and echoes its media. A relaying B2BUA or SBC handles two call legs per session.
- G.711 media is passed through; nothing is transcoded.
- Concurrent calls is the CPS times the 10 s call length, not a measured maximum; long-call capacity, where most SBC datasheets quote their concurrency, has not been measured yet.
- The server is a cloud VM, not an appliance.
- p50 and p99 setup are the RFC 6076 session request delay measured at 90% of max CPS, recorded in whole milliseconds (so 1 ms means 1 ms or less); n/m = not measured.
- The UDP + RTP max CPS was confirmed twice rather than three times.
- The release check also passed at 5% above the TLS and UDP signaling max CPS, so those two figures may be slightly understated.
- Not yet measured: B2BUA/relay mode (two legs per call), registrations, transcoding, WebRTC (DTLS-SRTP), and failover.
Full method and per-trial data: RFC 7501/7502 benchmark report.
1. Add one dependency.
[dependencies]
rvoip-sip = "0.5.1"
tokio = { version = "1", features = ["full"] }
Upgrading an existing application from 0.3? The 0.4 migration guide has before/after code for every breaking change.
2. Make a call. Bob answers, Alice dials, Alice hangs up. Everything runs on loopback, so this works on a laptop with no PBX, no account, and no network setup.
use std::time::Duration;
use rvoip_sip::{Config, Endpoint, EndpointProfile};
#[tokio::main]
async fn main() -> rvoip_sip::Result<()> {
    let bob = tokio::spawn(async {
        let mut bob = Endpoint::builder()
            .name("bob")
            .profile(EndpointProfile::Custom(Config::local("bob", 5071)))
            .build()
            .await?;
        let incoming = bob.wait_for_incoming().await?;
        let call = incoming.answer().await?;
        call.wait_for_end(None).await?;
        bob.shutdown().await
    });
    tokio::time::sleep(Duration::from_millis(300)).await;
    let alice = Endpoint::builder()
        .name("alice")
        .profile(EndpointProfile::Custom(Config::local("alice", 5070)))
        .build()
        .await?;
    let call = alice
        .call_and_wait("sip:bob@127.0.0.1:5071", Some(Duration::from_secs(10)))
        .await?;
    call.hangup_and_wait(Some(Duration::from_secs(5))).await?;
    alice.shutdown().await?;
    bob.await.unwrap()
}
3. Or run the same program from the checkout.
git clone https://github.com/eisenzopf/rvoip.git && cd rvoip
cargo run -p rvoip-sip --example endpoint_local_call
From here, examples/ walks forward one step at a time: real
microphone audio, registering to a PBX, hold/DTMF, transfers, SRTP and TLS, an
IVR, a B2BUA, then browser and voice-AI gateways.
The workspace contains 47 crates. You import one of them; it pulls in
everything it needs. The rest are internal layers you never name in your own
Cargo.toml.
Two shortcuts:
- rvoip-sip is the release-gated product. If your application is
telephony, start and stay there. It is the only crate with a signed
interoperability attestation (see evidence).
- rvoip is the facade. It re-exports rvoip-sip as rvoip::sip and adds
the shared Orchestrator plus optional transports and extensions behind
Cargo features. Its feature table
and the deployment bundles (bundle-sip-endpoint, bundle-carrier-sip, bundle-browser-gateway, bundle-ai-conversation, bundle-full-pure-rust, bundle-full-native) are the only feature lists you
need.
Deployment backends (Keycloak, LDAP, Redis, Postgres vCon storage, audit and
SIEM sinks, SAML, SCIM, WebAuthn, STIR/SHAKEN, IMS AKA) are separate
rvoip-* extension crates you add only when you operate that backend. The
extension catalog lists them.
For JavaScript UCTP control, see the experimental browser/Node WebSocket client. It is available as source with TypeScript declarations; the package is not yet published to npm. Application-profile dispatch is a separate opt-in server extension that stock servers do not enable.
Four API surfaces, from simplest to most control. Most applications use the first one.
Register an account on a PBX and place a call through it:
use std::time::Duration;
use rvoip_sip::{Endpoint, EndpointProfile, Result};
# async fn example() -> Result<()> {
let mut endpoint = Endpoint::builder()
    .name("alice")
    .account("1001")
    .password("secret")
    .registrar("sips:pbx.example.com:5061")
    .profile(EndpointProfile::AsteriskTlsSrtpRegisteredFlow)
    .build()
    .await?;
endpoint.register().await?;
let call = endpoint
    .call_and_wait("1002", Some(Duration::from_secs(30)))
    .await?;
call.send_dtmf('1').await?;
call.hangup_and_wait(Some(Duration::from_secs(5))).await?;
endpoint.shutdown().await?;
# Ok(())
# }
Answer calls and react to key presses, the shape of an IVR or auto-attendant:
use rvoip_sip::{CallHandlerDecision, CallbackPeer, Config, Result};
# async fn example() -> Result<()> {
let peer = CallbackPeer::builder(Config::local("ivr", 5120))
    .on_incoming(|call| async move {
        println!("incoming call from {}", call.from);
        CallHandlerDecision::Accept
    })
    .on_dtmf(|call, digit| async move {
        if digit == '0' {
            call.transfer_blind("sip:operator@127.0.0.1:5122").await?;
        }
        Ok(())
    })
    .on_ended(|call_id, reason| async move {
        println!("call {call_id} ended: {reason:?}");
        Ok(())
    })
    .build()
    .await?;
peer.run().await
# }
Security and transports are configuration, not different APIs: SDES-SRTP in
07-secure-call-srtp, TLS in
08-tls-transport, DTLS-SRTP, G.729, AMR-NB/WB,
and Opus behind Cargo features. The full surface is documented in the
SIP crate README.
RvoipApp declares transports, roles, and routing through one builder and
runs SIP, WebRTC, and UCTP listeners on the shared Orchestrator:
rvoip = { version = "0.5.1", features = ["app"] }use rvoip::app::*;
# async fn run() -> rvoip::app::AppResult<()> {
let app = RvoipApp::builder()
    .webrtc(
        WebRtcConfig::ws("127.0.0.1:8081")
            .allow(Role::Customer, [Capability::Text, Capability::Voice]),
    )
    .sip(
        SipConfig::bind("127.0.0.1:5060")
            .domain("callcenter.local")
            .allow(Role::Employee, [Capability::Voice])
            .registrar_users([("alice", "password123")]),
    )
    .employees(EmployeePolicy::named(["alice"]))
    .customers(CustomerPolicy::webrtc_only())
    .assignment(AssignmentPolicy::fixed("alice"))
    .on_message(|ctx, msg| async move {
        ctx.reply("Alice", format!("Alice received: {}", msg.text)).await
    })
    .build()
    .await?;
app.run().await
# }
Start from 12-customer-escalation-sip-webrtc:
a browser chat escalates to a SIP agent's phone. For SIP into Amazon Connect,
see 13-sip-to-amazon-connect.
- Hosted agent. rvoip-vapi speaks Vapi's
bidirectional raw-audio WebSocket directly. Your SIP or WebRTC caller is
bridged to the agent through the Orchestrator; the agent joins as its own
AI participant with typed events, control messages, and supervised teardown.
Start from 14-vapi-agent, one server that accepts
either kind of caller. Attaching an agent to a caller you already hold:
use std::sync::Arc; use rvoip::Orchestrator; use rvoip::core_traits::ids::ConnectionId; use rvoip::vapi::{VapiAdapter, VapiApiKey, VapiAssistant, VapiCallOptions, VapiConfig}; # async fn attach(orchestrator: Arc<Orchestrator>, caller: ConnectionId) # -> Result<(), Box<dyn std::error::Error>> { let key = VapiApiKey::new(std::env::var("VAPI_API_KEY")?)?; let adapter = VapiAdapter::new(VapiConfig::new(key))?; let options = VapiCallOptions::new(VapiAssistant::saved(std::env::var("VAPI_ASSISTANT_ID")?)); // Joins a distinct AI participant, originates the agent leg, bridges audio. let mut call = adapter.attach_agent(&orchestrator, caller, options).await?; call.say("One moment while I look that up.", false, true).await?; let _outcome = call.wait().await; # Ok(()) # }
- Your own pipeline. rvoip-harness gives you ASR, TTS, dialog, and recording provider traits, and rvoip-vcon emits signed vCon records of the
conversation. 11-ai-harness-demo runs the
whole loop with deterministic providers, so you can see the shape before you
plug in a real model.
Every transport turns into the same objects, so an application written against
the conversation model does not care whether a leg arrived over SIP, a browser,
or QUIC. That is what lets one Orchestrator bridge them.
┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│ Your application                                                  │
│ softphone · PBX · contact center · browser gateway · AI agent     │
└───────────────────────────────┬───────────────────────────────────┘
                                │  rvoip-sip  /  rvoip (+ features)
┌───────────────────────────────▼───────────────────────────────────┐
│ Shared conversation model  (rvoip-core)                           │
│ Orchestrator · Conversation · Session · Participant · Connection  │
│ routing · admission · bridges · media graph · events              │
└──────────────┬────────────────┬────────────────┬──────────────────┘
               │                │                │
        ┌──────▼──────┐  ┌──────▼──────┐  ┌──────▼───────────────┐
        │ SIP + RTP   │  │ WebRTC      │  │ UCTP                 │
        │ UDP/TCP/TLS │  │ ICE/DTLS    │  │ QUIC/WebTransport/WS │
        └──────┬──────┘  └──────┬──────┘  └──────┬───────────────┘
               └────────────────┼────────────────┘
                                │
             ┌──────────────────▼───────────────────┐
             │ Optional products and extensions      │
             │ Vapi · AI harness · vCon · MoQ        │
             │ Amazon Connect · identity · audit     │
             └───────────────────────────────────────┘
Words you will meet in the API:
- Conversation — the thing people are in together. It outlives any one connection.
- Session — one participant's time in a conversation.
- Participant — a human or an AI agent. Roles (Agent, Observer,
customer) can change without moving connections.
- Connection — one transport leg: a SIP dialog, a browser peer connection, a UCTP session.
- Stream — the audio (or video, or data) flowing on a connection. Bridges join streams across connections, transcoding when codecs differ.
Adapters depend on the shared ConnectionAdapter trait; the core never
imports a transport. The design docs go deeper, starting with
INTERFACE_DESIGN.md and
CONVERSATION_PROTOCOL.md.
Beta-qualified means covered by the SIP release gate and its interoperability, security, standards, performance, and soak evidence. Developer preview means implemented and published, but API-unstable or outside that attestation. Planned items appear only in the roadmap. A published crate or Cargo feature is evidence of availability, not a blanket production-readiness statement.
Capability detail by area (click to expand)
The WebRTC implementation of ICE and DTLS-SRTP is separate from the SIP beta claim. UCTP and MoQ availability does not imply that SIP-over-QUIC or RTP-over-QUIC has shipped.
All 14 extension crates ship at 0.5.1. They stay optional so protocol
crates depend on provider contracts, never on a deployment backend.
How they attach: voip-3 on the facade brings in the harness, vCon, and the
identity surface; vapi and sip-stir-shaken are their own facade features;
everything else is a direct dependency.
rvoip = { version = "0.5.1", features = ["sip", "vapi", "sip-stir-shaken"] }
rvoip-keycloak = "0.5.1"
rvoip-redis = "0.5.1"
rvoip-audit = "0.5.1"
The contracts they implement live in
rvoip-auth-core,
rvoip-users-core, and
rvoip-identity.
For orientation only. Everything below the front doors is a transitive
dependency of rvoip or rvoip-sip; depend on it directly only when you are
extending the platform itself.
The full release path exercises rvoip-sip public APIs against independently
implemented peers. The exact peer versions, scenarios, transports, results, and
exclusions are recorded in signed qualification evidence; see the
compatibility matrix.
Patch releases can explicitly use the shorter release path: unit, integration, regression, example, and doc tests, including runnable TURN/ICE examples, without benchmark, soak/load, or extended interoperability jobs. Each GitHub release links its tested commit and gate results; a reduced patch report marks performance qualification NOT RUN. The matrix below describes the full qualification scope, not a claim that a patch run repeated it.
The strict full-beta gate requires an explicit PASS attestation for every required independently managed peer below. The report generator binds each row to the tested source tree, exact peer identity and configuration, selected matrix, and hashed evidence; it refuses to produce a strict release-candidate report if a required peer is missing, skipped, ambiguous, unpinned, or failing.
The 0.5.1 release uses the shorter patch profile: regular unit, integration, regression, example, and doc tests, including TURN/ICE checks. Benchmarks and extended external interoperability were not rerun. See the 0.5.1 release notes and the release's qualification attachments for exact commands, results, and scope.
Historical full-release evidence, retained at its original versions:
- Qualification report — exact versions, row counts, scenarios, hashes, PASS status.
- Performance report.
- RFC 7501/7502 benchmark report: sustained Session Establishment Rates for published 0.5.0 on one c8i.4xlarge, reported in IETF format (R1 TLS 8,348 and R5 UDP 8,256 sessions/s without media; R2 TLS+SRTP 1,124 and R6 UDP+RTP 1,204 with media).
- RFC evidence matrix and security posture.
- Interop plan — the evidence boundaries.
- Changelog, release notes and the 0.4 migration guide — what changed in this release and how to upgrade from 0.3.
This is bounded interoperability evidence, not a claim of compatibility with every version, module, configuration, transport, codec, or SIP extension, and not carrier certification. Anything outside a stated qualification boundary remains the application's responsibility to validate in its own deployment.
Developer-preview products document their own supported scope and gaps in their crate READMEs. A published crate or Cargo feature is evidence of availability, not a blanket production-readiness statement.
git clone https://github.com/eisenzopf/rvoip.git
cd rvoip
# Build the default workspace members
cargo build
# Run a working SIP call
cargo run -p rvoip-sip --example endpoint_local_call
# Run the workspace test suite
scripts/test_all.sh
Feature-gated targets (codecs, DTLS-SRTP, generated compliance suites) only
build with their features on; use --all-features when you touch a gated
path. docs/RELEASING.md describes the release train.
Remaining major items, beyond the developer-preview products above:
- SIP-over-QUIC and RTP-over-QUIC (RoQ) transport profiles.
- Integrated multi-party SFU/MCU products beyond the existing media primitives.
- Production graduation of AAuth as its standards work and deployment evidence mature.
- Deeper AI participants with multi-agent orchestration beyond today's provider harness.
- Additional qualification and release gates for the developer-preview products.
Detailed engineering gaps are tracked in docs/GAP_PLAN.md
and in issues.
- Bugs: open an issue with reproduction steps.
- Feature requests: use discussions or issues and describe the target product and compatibility expectations.
- Pull requests: workspace-wide tests run through scripts/test_all.sh.
