Rvoip 0.5 the complete VoIP stack in Rust
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-26
19:18:09 UTC
04:18:09 KST

A Rust library for building communications applications. SIP, RTP, WebRTC, QUIC/WebTransport/WebSocket conversations, Media over QUIC, and voice-AI agents share one call model, and the whole stack is in the box: no external SIP server, media server, or RTP engine to install and wire together.
Five-minute start · For carriers · Which crate? · Pick a path · How it fits together · What ships · Interop evidence · Changes · Upgrading from 0.3
rvoip is a library, not a server you deploy next to your application. Signalling, media, codecs, NAT traversal, security, and the conversation model are all Rust crates in this workspace, so a call goes from your code to the wire without a separately installed SIP proxy, media server, or RTP engine. The examples below run on a laptop with nothing installed but Rust; the same crates carry the release-gated interoperability evidence against Asterisk, FreeSWITCH, Jambonz, Kamailio, and OpenSIPS. Carriers evaluating standards coverage, performance, and reliability should start with For carriers.
One AWS c8i.4xlarge (16 vCPU, 8 cores, Intel Xeon 6, 32 GiB) running a single rvoip process, measured with the RFC 7501/7502 benchmark:
At 2x capacity, rvoip keeps admitting calls at its max CPS, refuses the excess with fast 503 responses, and recovers cleanly once the load drops.
- Max CPS (calls per second; RFC 7502's Session Establishment Rate) is the highest rate with zero failed calls over a trial of at least 180 s and 50,000 call attempts.
- rvoip was measured as a terminating endpoint: it answers each call and echoes its media. A relaying B2BUA or SBC handles two call legs per session.
- G.711 media is passed through; nothing is transcoded.
- Concurrent calls is the CPS times the 10 s call length, not a measured maximum; long-call capacity, where most SBC datasheets quote their concurrency, has not been measured yet.
- The server is a cloud VM, not an appliance.
- p50 and p99 setup are the RFC 6076 session request delay measured at 90% of max CPS, recorded in whole milliseconds (so 1 ms means 1 ms or less); n/m = not measured.
- The UDP + RTP max CPS was confirmed twice rather than three times.
- The release check also passed at 5% above the TLS and UDP signaling max CPS, so those two figures may be slightly understated.
- Not yet measured: B2BUA/relay mode (two legs per call), registrations, transcoding, WebRTC (DTLS-SRTP), and failover.
Full method and per-trial data: RFC 7501/7502 benchmark report.
1. Add one dependency.
[dependencies]
rvoip-sip = "0.5.1"
tokio = { version = "1", features = ["full"] }
Upgrading an existing application from 0.3? The 0.4 migration guide has before/after code for every breaking change.
2. Make a call. Bob answers, Alice dials, Alice hangs up. Everything runs on loopback, so this works on a laptop with no PBX, no account, and no network setup.
use std::time::Duration;
use rvoip_sip::{Config, Endpoint, EndpointProfile};
#[tokio::main]
async fn main() -> rvoip_sip::Result<()> {
    let bob = tokio::spawn(async {
        let mut bob = Endpoint::builder()
            .name("bob")
            .profile(EndpointProfile::Custom(Config::local("bob", 5071)))
            .build()
            .await?;
        let incoming = bob.wait_for_incoming().await?;
        let call = incoming.answer().await?;
        call.wait_for_end(None).await?;
        bob.shutdown().await
    });
    tokio::time::sleep(Duration::from_millis(300)).await;
    let alice = Endpoint::builder()
        .name("alice")
        .profile(EndpointProfile::Custom(Config::local("alice", 5070)))
        .build()
        .await?;
    let call = alice
        .call_and_wait("sip:bob@127.0.0.1:5071", Some(Duration::from_secs(10)))
        .await?;
    call.hangup_and_wait(Some(Duration::from_secs(5))).await?;
    alice.shutdown().await?;
    bob.await.unwrap()
}
3. Or run the same program from the checkout.
git clone https://github.com/eisenzopf/rvoip.git && cd rvoip
cargo run -p rvoip-sip --example endpoint_local_call
From here, examples/ walks forward one step at a time: real
microphone audio, registering to a PBX, hold/DTMF, transfers, SRTP and TLS, an
IVR, a B2BUA, then browser and voice-AI gateways.
The workspace contains 47 crates. You import one of them; it pulls in
everything it needs. The rest are internal layers you never name in your own
Cargo.toml.
Two shortcuts:
- rvoip-sip is the release-gated product. If your application is
telephony, start and stay there. It is the only crate with a signed
interoperability attestation (see evidence).
- rvoip is the facade. It re-exports rvoip-sip as rvoip::sip and adds
the shared Orchestrator plus optional transports and extensions behind
Cargo features. Its feature table
and the deployment bundles (bundle-sip-endpoint, bundle-carrier-sip, bundle-browser-gateway, bundle-ai-conversation, bundle-full-pure-rust, bundle-full-native) are the only feature lists you
need.
Deployment backends (Keycloak, LDAP, Redis, Postgres vCon storage, audit and
SIEM sinks, SAML, SCIM, WebAuthn, STIR/SHAKEN, IMS AKA) are separate
rvoip-* extension crates you add only when you operate that backend. The
extension catalog lists them.
For JavaScript UCTP control, see the experimental browser/Node WebSocket client. It is available as source with TypeScript declarations; the package is not yet published to npm. Application-profile dispatch is a separate opt-in server extension that stock servers do not enable.
Four API surfaces, from simplest to most control. Most applications use the first one.
Register an account on a PBX and place a call through it:
use std::time::Duration;
use rvoip_sip::{Endpoint, EndpointProfile, Result};
# async fn example() -> Result<()> {
let mut endpoint = Endpoint::builder()
    .name("alice")
    .account("1001")
    .password("secret")
    .registrar("sips:pbx.example.com:5061")
    .profile(EndpointProfile::AsteriskTlsSrtpRegisteredFlow)
    .build()
    .await?;
endpoint.register().await?;
let call = endpoint
    .call_and_wait("1002", Some(Duration::from_secs(30)))
    .await?;
call.send_dtmf('1').await?;
call.hangup_and_wait(Some(Duration::from_secs(5))).await?;
endpoint.shutdown().await?;
# Ok(())
# }
Answer calls and react to key presses, the shape of an IVR or auto-attendant:
use rvoip_sip::{CallHandlerDecision, CallbackPeer, Config, Result};
# async fn example() -> Result<()> {
let peer = CallbackPeer::builder(Config::local("ivr", 5120))
    .on_incoming(|call| async move {
        println!("incoming call from {}", call.from);
        CallHandlerDecision::Accept
    })
    .on_dtmf(|call, digit| async move {
        if digit == '0' {
            call.transfer_blind("sip:operator@127.0.0.1:5122").await?;
        }
        Ok(())
    })
    .on_ended(|call_id, reason| async move {
        println!("call {call_id} ended: {reason:?}");
        Ok(())
    })
    .build()
    .await?;
peer.run().await
# }
Security and transports are configuration, not different APIs: SDES-SRTP in
07-secure-call-srtp, TLS in
08-tls-transport, DTLS-SRTP, G.729, AMR-NB/WB,
and Opus behind Cargo features. The full surface is documented in the
SIP crate README.
RvoipApp declares transports, roles, and routing through one builder and
runs SIP, WebRTC, and UCTP listeners on the shared Orchestrator:
rvoip = { version = "0.5.1", features = ["app"] }use rvoip::app::*;
# async fn run() -> rvoip::app::AppResult<()> {
let app = RvoipApp::builder()
    .webrtc(
        WebRtcConfig::ws("127.0.0.1:8081")
            .allow(Role::Customer, [Capability::Text, Capability::Voice]),
    )
    .sip(
        SipConfig::bind("127.0.0.1:5060")
            .domain("callcenter.local")
            .allow(Role::Employee, [Capability::Voice])
            .registrar_users([("alice", "password123")]),
    )
    .employees(EmployeePolicy::named(["alice"]))
    .customers(CustomerPolicy::webrtc_only())
    .assignment(AssignmentPolicy::fixed("alice"))
    .on_message(|ctx, msg| async move {
        ctx.reply("Alice", format!("Alice received: {}", msg.text)).await
    })
    .build()
    .await?;
app.run().await
# }
Start from 12-customer-escalation-sip-webrtc:
a browser chat escalates to a SIP agent's phone. For SIP into Amazon Connect,
see 13-sip-to-amazon-connect.
- Hosted agent. rvoip-vapi speaks Vapi's
bidirectional raw-audio WebSocket directly. Your SIP or WebRTC caller is
bridged to the agent through the Orchestrator; the agent joins as its own
AI participant with typed events, control messages, and supervised teardown.
Start from 14-vapi-agent, one server that accepts
either kind of caller. Attaching an agent to a caller you already hold:
use std::sync::Arc; use rvoip::Orchestrator; use rvoip::core_traits::ids::ConnectionId; use rvoip::vapi::{VapiAdapter, VapiApiKey, VapiAssistant, VapiCallOptions, VapiConfig}; # async fn attach(orchestrator: Arc<Orchestrator>, caller: ConnectionId) # -> Result<(), Box<dyn std::error::Error>> { let key = VapiApiKey::new(std::env::var("VAPI_API_KEY")?)?; let adapter = VapiAdapter::new(VapiConfig::new(key))?; let options = VapiCallOptions::new(VapiAssistant::saved(std::env::var("VAPI_ASSISTANT_ID")?)); // Joins a distinct AI participant, originates the agent leg, bridges audio. let mut call = adapter.attach_agent(&orchestrator, caller, options).await?; call.say("One moment while I look that up.", false, true).await?; let _outcome = call.wait().await; # Ok(()) # }
- Your own pipeline. rvoip-harness gives you ASR, TTS, dialog, and recording provider traits, and rvoip-vcon emits signed vCon records of the
conversation. 11-ai-harness-demo runs the
whole loop with deterministic providers, so you can see the shape before you
plug in a real model.
Every transport turns into the same objects, so an application written against
the conversation model does not care whether a leg arrived over SIP, a browser,
or QUIC. That is what lets one Orchestrator bridge them.
┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│ Your application                                                  │
│ softphone · PBX · contact center · browser gateway · AI agent     │
└───────────────────────────────┬───────────────────────────────────┘
                                │  rvoip-sip  /  rvoip (+ features)
┌───────────────────────────────▼───────────────────────────────────┐
│ Shared conversation model  (rvoip-core)                           │
│ Orchestrator · Conversation · Session · Participant · Connection  │
│ routing · admission · bridges · media graph · events              │
└──────────────┬────────────────┬────────────────┬──────────────────┘
               │                │                │
        ┌──────▼──────┐  ┌──────▼──────┐  ┌──────▼───────────────┐
        │ SIP + RTP   │  │ WebRTC      │  │ UCTP                 │
        │ UDP/TCP/TLS │  │ ICE/DTLS    │  │ QUIC/WebTransport/WS │
        └──────┬──────┘  └──────┬──────┘  └──────┬───────────────┘
               └────────────────┼────────────────┘
                                │
             ┌──────────────────▼───────────────────┐
             │ Optional products and extensions      │
             │ Vapi · AI harness · vCon · MoQ        │
             │ Amazon Connect · identity · audit     │
             └───────────────────────────────────────┘
Words you will meet in the API:
- Conversation — the thing people are in together. It outlives any one connection.
- Session — one participant's time in a conversation.
- Participant — a human or an AI agent. Roles (Agent, Observer,
customer) can change without moving connections.
- Connection — one transport leg: a SIP dialog, a browser peer connection, a UCTP session.
- Stream — the audio (or video, or data) flowing on a connection. Bridges join streams across connections, transcoding when codecs differ.
Adapters depend on the shared ConnectionAdapter trait; the core never
imports a transport. The design docs go deeper, starting with
INTERFACE_DESIGN.md and
CONVERSATION_PROTOCOL.md.
Beta-qualified means covered by the SIP release gate and its interoperability, security, standards, performance, and soak evidence. Developer preview means implemented and published, but API-unstable or outside that attestation. Planned items appear only in the roadmap. A published crate or Cargo feature is evidence of availability, not a blanket production-readiness statement.
Capability detail by area (click to expand)
The WebRTC implementation of ICE and DTLS-SRTP is separate from the SIP beta claim. UCTP and MoQ availability does not imply that SIP-over-QUIC or RTP-over-QUIC has shipped.
All 14 extension crates ship at 0.5.1. They stay optional so protocol
crates depend on provider contracts, never on a deployment backend.
How they attach: voip-3 on the facade brings in the harness, vCon, and the
identity surface; vapi and sip-stir-shaken are their own facade features;
everything else is a direct dependency.
rvoip = { version = "0.5.1", features = ["sip", "vapi", "sip-stir-shaken"] }
rvoip-keycloak = "0.5.1"
rvoip-redis = "0.5.1"
rvoip-audit = "0.5.1"
The contracts they implement live in
rvoip-auth-core,
rvoip-users-core, and
rvoip-identity.
For orientation only. Everything below the front doors is a transitive
dependency of rvoip or rvoip-sip; depend on it directly only when you are
extending the platform itself.
The full release path exercises rvoip-sip public APIs against independently
implemented peers. The exact peer versions, scenarios, transports, results, and
exclusions are recorded in signed qualification evidence; see the
compatibility matrix.
Patch releases can explicitly use the shorter release path: unit, integration, regression, example, and doc tests, including runnable TURN/ICE examples, without benchmark, soak/load, or extended interoperability jobs. Each GitHub release links its tested commit and gate results; a reduced patch report marks performance qualification NOT RUN. The matrix below describes the full qualification scope, not a claim that a patch run repeated it.
The strict full-beta gate requires an explicit PASS attestation for every required independently managed peer below. The report generator binds each row to the tested source tree, exact peer identity and configuration, selected matrix, and hashed evidence; it refuses to produce a strict release-candidate report if a required peer is missing, skipped, ambiguous, unpinned, or failing.
The 0.5.1 release uses the shorter patch profile: regular unit, integration, regression, example, and doc tests, including TURN/ICE checks. Benchmarks and extended external interoperability were not rerun. See the 0.5.1 release notes and the release's qualification attachments for exact commands, results, and scope.
Historical full-release evidence, retained at its original versions:
- Qualification report — exact versions, row counts, scenarios, hashes, PASS status.
- Performance report.
- RFC 7501/7502 benchmark report: sustained Session Establishment Rates for published 0.5.0 on one c8i.4xlarge, reported in IETF format (R1 TLS 8,348 and R5 UDP 8,256 sessions/s without media; R2 TLS+SRTP 1,124 and R6 UDP+RTP 1,204 with media).
- RFC evidence matrix and security posture.
- Interop plan — the evidence boundaries.
- Changelog, release notes and the 0.4 migration guide — what changed in this release and how to upgrade from 0.3.
This is bounded interoperability evidence, not a claim of compatibility with every version, module, configuration, transport, codec, or SIP extension, and not carrier certification. Anything outside a stated qualification boundary remains the application's responsibility to validate in its own deployment.
Developer-preview products document their own supported scope and gaps in their crate READMEs. A published crate or Cargo feature is evidence of availability, not a blanket production-readiness statement.
git clone https://github.com/eisenzopf/rvoip.git
cd rvoip
# Build the default workspace members
cargo build
# Run a working SIP call
cargo run -p rvoip-sip --example endpoint_local_call
# Run the workspace test suite
scripts/test_all.sh
Feature-gated targets (codecs, DTLS-SRTP, generated compliance suites) only
build with their features on; use --all-features when you touch a gated
path. docs/RELEASING.md describes the release train.
Remaining major items, beyond the developer-preview products above:
- SIP-over-QUIC and RTP-over-QUIC (RoQ) transport profiles.
- Integrated multi-party SFU/MCU products beyond the existing media primitives.
- Production graduation of AAuth as its standards work and deployment evidence mature.
- Deeper AI participants with multi-agent orchestration beyond today's provider harness.
- Additional qualification and release gates for the developer-preview products.
Detailed engineering gaps are tracked in docs/GAP_PLAN.md
and in issues.
- Bugs: open an issue with reproduction steps.
- Feature requests: use discussions or issues and describe the target product and compatibility expectations.
- Pull requests: workspace-wide tests run through scripts/test_all.sh.

Show HN: Fuck_data_brokers.py
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-26
18:37:21 UTC
03:37:21 KST

"Today was funny: I was dealing with some scumbag data broker this morning, trying to get a good friend of mine's information deleted, and they WOULDN'T do it. No matter what I tried. I felt so ugh! I just wanted to reach through the screen and punch them in the face. Well, Mr. Data Broker, here's me reaching through the screen and punching you in the face. Enjoy f*ck_data_brokers.py"
I remove people's data from data brokers. It is one of my main part-time jobs, and it has been a wild ride.
All I want to do is help people. In the world of AI that has become next to impossible. From where I sit, data brokers have ramped up production almost 1000x since GPT-4 came out. Every scraper got smarter, every profile got fatter, and every "people search" site got a hundred cousins.
Then it hit me: I do not have a trillion dollars. By my rough estimate that is what it would cost in compute to clean up everyone's data for them, properly and automatically. I cannot do it for everyone.
I am fed up with what everyone is put through. So here is my response:
f*ck_data_brokers.py
One Python file. No dependencies. Free. It runs on your computer, keeps your details on your computer, and makes data brokers delete you.
Every star is one more person these companies have to answer. The more stars, the sooner they learn their lesson.
It costs you one click. It costs them a compliance department.
This tool is free and always will be. It is also work: 600 companies, their excuses, their forms, their silence, and then next month they collect you again.
Agent Garrett is the service I built to do all of it for you. Set it and forget it(you can do it for free with this tool if you cannot afford the subscription!).
Persistence is guaranteed. Agent Garrett does not get bored, does not get tired and does not take "use our form" for an answer. A broker can stall a person. It cannot outlast a machine that never stops asking, and that is how removal happens: not with one letter, but with the one that never stops coming.
Data brokers are companies you have never heard of. They collect your name, your address, your phone number, your relatives and your habits, without asking. They sell all of it to anyone with a credit card. Then they write "we value your privacy" at the bottom of the page and expect you to keep a straight face.
More than 600 of them signed a government registry admitting, in writing, that selling people is their business. That makes them very easy to find.
- Gets the list of registered data brokers. All 600 and more.
- Writes each one a real legal deletion and opt-out letter in your name, quoting the law where you live.
- Sends the letters from your own free mailbox, or hands them to you ready to send.
- Reads what they write back and tells you which excuse it is and what to answer.
- Chases the ones that go quiet and tells you where to file a complaint about the ones that refuse.
The tool is rude about data brokers. The letters are not. They are firm, correct and legal, because the goal is to leave a broker with nothing to hide behind.
- Python 3.8 or newer. Check with python --version. If you do not have it, get it free from python.org.
- An email address. A free one is fine.
That is all. No account, no subscription, no AI required.
Download fuck_data_brokers.py, open a terminal in that folder, and run:
python fuck_data_brokers.py
You get a menu. Go down the list:
- Tell it who you are. Name, email, city, and where you live. No street address, no birthday, no ID. Brokers get the minimum.
- Get the list of data brokers.
- Write the deletion letters. It writes 20 at a time.
- Send them. See below.
- Come back when a broker answers, and pick A broker answered. What now? Paste the reply. It tells you what they are up to and writes your answer.
- Now and then, pick Chase the quiet ones. They will be quiet. They are counting on you giving up.
Every menu item is also a command, if you prefer: python fuck_data_brokers.py --help.
You do not need to pay for an email service. Pick whichever is easier.
The send page (no setup at all). After writing letters, the tool gives you a page called send.html. Open it in your browser. Each letter has a button that opens it in your email, already addressed and written. Press send. Next. If the button does not open your email, the page also has copy buttons for the address, the subject and the letter. When you are done, run python fuck_data_brokers.py sent all so the clock starts.
Let the tool send them. Run python fuck_data_brokers.py send. It sends through your own mailbox and knows the settings for Gmail, Outlook, Yahoo and iCloud. It asks for an app password, which you create in your email account's security settings after turning on two-step sign-in. The password is used once and never saved.
A separate free address just for this is a good idea. Their replies stay out of your real inbox.
One command does everything that needs no decision from you:
python fuck_data_brokers.py auto
It refreshes the broker list, writes the next 20 letters, writes reminders for brokers that are late, and rebuilds your send page. Run it as often as you like; it never writes to the same broker twice. Add --send and it also sends, using the app password in the FDB_SMTP_PASS environment variable.
Run it once a week and the whole list is covered in a few months without you thinking about it. On Linux or macOS, add a line to your crontab. On Windows, create a weekly task in Task Scheduler that runs the same command.
0 9 * * 1  python3 /path/to/fuck_data_brokers.py auto
The one thing it cannot do for you is read your inbox. When a broker answers, paste the reply into reply.
Some will not act on an email. They want you on their web form, behind a CAPTCHA, because a company that scrapes the internet with bots would like to be sure you are not a bot.
Run python fuck_data_brokers.py forms. You get a page with a link to each broker's own form and copy buttons for exactly what to paste. You fill it in, you do the CAPTCHA, you press submit. Then tell the tool: python fuck_data_brokers.py sent req-1a2b3c4d.
If a form demands a photo of your ID, close the tab. They sell personal data. They are not getting more of it from you.
Then tell the tool, so the request does not die there:
python fuck_data_brokers.py noid req-1a2b3c4d
The request carries on in writing without the form. See What happens when I refuse to send ID?
The tool is also an MCP server, so an AI assistant that supports MCP can do the boring part for you. Add this to your AI app's MCP settings, with the real path to the file:
{
  "mcpServers": {
    "fuck_data_brokers": {
      "command": "python",
      "args": ["/path/to/fuck_data_brokers.py", "mcp"]
    }
  }
}
Then say: "Use fuck_data_brokers to get me deleted from data brokers."
Your AI will ask who you are, fetch the broker list, write the letters, read the replies you give it, and write the answers. It is told to ask you before sending anything, never to invent your details, never to send an ID, and never to click a link from a broker.
If your AI has a browser, it can fill in a broker's form while you watch. You still do the CAPTCHA and press submit yourself.
They used AI to build a file on you. Use AI to make them delete it.
A broker that refuses twice gets a final notice with seven days to act. After that, the tool tells you which privacy regulator takes complaints for your law and how to file. Regulators read those.
- Send your street address, your birthday or your ID.
- Click a link in a broker's email.
- Get past a CAPTCHA for you.
- Write to a broker twice with the same request.
- Tell you that you are deleted when all it knows is that a letter was sent.
The letter quotes the law of the place you live. You pick it once, in setup.
In the EU, UK or Brazil? The broker list comes from US registries, because the US is where brokers are made to register. Your law still reaches them: the GDPR covers a company outside the EU that offers services to people in the EU or tracks what they do there (Article 3(2)), and Brazil's law covers a company that processes the data of people in Brazil (Article 3). Add any broker from your own country with the add command.
In California? Use the state's own free service too: DROP. One request there reaches every broker registered in California, and they have to check it again at least every 45 days. This tool still covers brokers registered elsewhere, and it reads and answers what brokers write back.
Sometimes, yes. That is the honest trade-off, and you should make it with your eyes open.
A deletion request has to say who you are, or the broker cannot find you. Some of the companies on the list will hold nothing on you until your letter arrives. Here is what the tool does about it:
- It sends three things and no more: your name, your email address and your city. No street address, no birthday, no phone number, no ID.
- Every letter tells the broker not to use those details for anything except the request.
- You choose who gets a letter. Look before you write, write to some, leave others out:
python fuck_data_brokers.py find "people"
python fuck_data_brokers.py letters --match "people"
python fuck_data_brokers.py skip "advertising"
- The law is on your side, up to a point. In California, information a business collects to verify your request may be used only for that (Civil Code 1798.130(a)(7)). In the EU, the general rule that data is used only for the purpose it was given for applies here too (GDPR Article 5(1)(b)). Neither stops a dishonest company. Both give you something to complain about.
If you want to be careful, start with the brokers most likely to have you, such as the people-search sites, and skip the rest.
The broker will try to treat that as the end of the matter. It is not.
Run noid with the request's reference and the tool keeps going:
- It writes to the broker's privacy address, says you will not send an identity document, and asks them to verify you from what they already hold. At the very least it asks them to stop selling and sharing your data, which gives nothing away to the person asking.
- If they refuse again, they get a final notice with seven days to act.
- After that, the tool tells you which regulator takes complaints for your law and how to file.
A broker that publishes no email address and whose only form demands ID has refused you by design. For those, the tool goes straight to step 3.
One fair point for the brokers: a company with real doubts about who is asking may ask for something more to confirm it (in the EU, GDPR Article 12(6)). That is not the same as demanding a photo of your passport. In California, a business may not require a verified request at all for an opt-out of sale or sharing (11 CCR section 7026).
It gets the request made properly, answered, chased and escalated. Whether a broker really deleted you is the broker's word, and the tool says so: it reports "they say deleted", not "deleted".
In one file, state.json, in a folder called .fuck_data_brokers in your home directory. Letters waiting to be sent are in outbox beside it. Nothing is uploaded anywhere. Delete the folder and it is gone.
The list comes from official public registries, where companies have to register as data brokers:
- the California Privacy Protection Agency
- the Oregon Division of Financial Regulation
- the Texas Secretary of State
Together that is more than 750 companies. You do not have to live in any of those states to write to them. A company registered in more than one place is written to once.
A job in this repository checks California's registry every hour. When a new broker registers there, it is on the list within the hour, and the commit says who it was. Oregon and Texas do not allow programs to fetch their lists, so those are downloaded by hand from the official pages and added with update-list --from.
The tool also fetches California's live registry when you ask for the list. A copy of everything, brokers.json, ships with the tool.
Know a broker that is on no registry? Open an issue with its name, its privacy page or privacy email, and a link showing that it sells or shares personal information. Reviewed reports go into more_brokers.json. Nothing gets on the list because a program guessed.
You can also add any broker for yourself:
python fuck_data_brokers.py add "Some Broker" privacy@somebroker.example
Two kinds of help are worth the most:
- A place that is missing. Adding a law is a few lines in one file: an entry in PLACES, the letter wording in LAW_TEXT, the complaint route in REGULATOR, and a test. Quote the law's own text and link to it. Wording from memory will not be merged.
- A registry that is missing. If your country or state publishes a list of data brokers, open an issue with the link. If its website allows programs to fetch the file, it can be checked every hour.
Know a broker that is on no registry? See The broker list.
This repository looks after itself:
- Every hour it checks for newly registered brokers and commits them.
- Every push and pull request runs the tests on Linux, Windows and macOS. The tests include a leak check, so a change that adds a path from someone's computer, a real email address, a phone number or a key cannot be merged.
- Every month it opens an issue if a hand-kept registry list has gone stale.
- Every week it checks whether the parts its jobs are built from have newer versions.
python -m unittest
They use made-up people and brokers. Nothing is sent and nothing is downloaded.
MIT. Take it, change it, share it. The broker list is a public record published by the State of California and is not covered by this license.

Show HN: Fuck_data_brokers.py
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-26
18:37:21 UTC
03:37:21 KST

"Today was funny: I was dealing with some scumbag data broker this morning, trying to get a good friend of mine's information deleted, and they WOULDN'T do it. No matter what I tried. I felt so ugh! I just wanted to reach through the screen and punch them in the face. Well, Mr. Data Broker, here's me reaching through the screen and punching you in the face. Enjoy f*ck_data_brokers.py"
I remove people's data from data brokers. It is one of my main part-time jobs, and it has been a wild ride.
All I want to do is help people. In the world of AI that has become next to impossible. From where I sit, data brokers have ramped up production almost 1000x since GPT-4 came out. Every scraper got smarter, every profile got fatter, and every "people search" site got a hundred cousins.
Then it hit me: I do not have a trillion dollars. By my rough estimate that is what it would cost in compute to clean up everyone's data for them, properly and automatically. I cannot do it for everyone.
I am fed up with what everyone is put through. So here is my response:
f*ck_data_brokers.py
One Python file. No dependencies. Free. It runs on your computer, keeps your details on your computer, and makes data brokers delete you.
Every star is one more person these companies have to answer. The more stars, the sooner they learn their lesson.
It costs you one click. It costs them a compliance department.
This tool is free and always will be. It is also work: 600 companies, their excuses, their forms, their silence, and then next month they collect you again.
Agent Garrett is the service I built to do all of it for you. Set it and forget it(you can do it for free with this tool if you cannot afford the subscription!).
Persistence is guaranteed. Agent Garrett does not get bored, does not get tired and does not take "use our form" for an answer. A broker can stall a person. It cannot outlast a machine that never stops asking, and that is how removal happens: not with one letter, but with the one that never stops coming.
Data brokers are companies you have never heard of. They collect your name, your address, your phone number, your relatives and your habits, without asking. They sell all of it to anyone with a credit card. Then they write "we value your privacy" at the bottom of the page and expect you to keep a straight face.
More than 600 of them signed a government registry admitting, in writing, that selling people is their business. That makes them very easy to find.
- Gets the list of registered data brokers. All 600 and more.
- Writes each one a real legal deletion and opt-out letter in your name, quoting the law where you live.
- Sends the letters from your own free mailbox, or hands them to you ready to send.
- Reads what they write back and tells you which excuse it is and what to answer.
- Chases the ones that go quiet and tells you where to file a complaint about the ones that refuse.
The tool is rude about data brokers. The letters are not. They are firm, correct and legal, because the goal is to leave a broker with nothing to hide behind.
- Python 3.8 or newer. Check with python --version. If you do not have it, get it free from python.org.
- An email address. A free one is fine.
That is all. No account, no subscription, no AI required.
Download fuck_data_brokers.py, open a terminal in that folder, and run:
python fuck_data_brokers.py
You get a menu. Go down the list:
- Tell it who you are. Name, email, city, and where you live. No street address, no birthday, no ID. Brokers get the minimum.
- Get the list of data brokers.
- Write the deletion letters. It writes 20 at a time.
- Send them. See below.
- Come back when a broker answers, and pick A broker answered. What now? Paste the reply. It tells you what they are up to and writes your answer.
- Now and then, pick Chase the quiet ones. They will be quiet. They are counting on you giving up.
Every menu item is also a command, if you prefer: python fuck_data_brokers.py --help.
You do not need to pay for an email service. Pick whichever is easier.
The send page (no setup at all). After writing letters, the tool gives you a page called send.html. Open it in your browser. Each letter has a button that opens it in your email, already addressed and written. Press send. Next. If the button does not open your email, the page also has copy buttons for the address, the subject and the letter. When you are done, run python fuck_data_brokers.py sent all so the clock starts.
Let the tool send them. Run python fuck_data_brokers.py send. It sends through your own mailbox and knows the settings for Gmail, Outlook, Yahoo and iCloud. It asks for an app password, which you create in your email account's security settings after turning on two-step sign-in. The password is used once and never saved.
A separate free address just for this is a good idea. Their replies stay out of your real inbox.
One command does everything that needs no decision from you:
python fuck_data_brokers.py auto
It refreshes the broker list, writes the next 20 letters, writes reminders for brokers that are late, and rebuilds your send page. Run it as often as you like; it never writes to the same broker twice. Add --send and it also sends, using the app password in the FDB_SMTP_PASS environment variable.
Run it once a week and the whole list is covered in a few months without you thinking about it. On Linux or macOS, add a line to your crontab. On Windows, create a weekly task in Task Scheduler that runs the same command.
0 9 * * 1  python3 /path/to/fuck_data_brokers.py auto
The one thing it cannot do for you is read your inbox. When a broker answers, paste the reply into reply.
Some will not act on an email. They want you on their web form, behind a CAPTCHA, because a company that scrapes the internet with bots would like to be sure you are not a bot.
Run python fuck_data_brokers.py forms. You get a page with a link to each broker's own form and copy buttons for exactly what to paste. You fill it in, you do the CAPTCHA, you press submit. Then tell the tool: python fuck_data_brokers.py sent req-1a2b3c4d.
If a form demands a photo of your ID, close the tab. They sell personal data. They are not getting more of it from you.
Then tell the tool, so the request does not die there:
python fuck_data_brokers.py noid req-1a2b3c4d
The request carries on in writing without the form. See What happens when I refuse to send ID?
The tool is also an MCP server, so an AI assistant that supports MCP can do the boring part for you. Add this to your AI app's MCP settings, with the real path to the file:
{
  "mcpServers": {
    "fuck_data_brokers": {
      "command": "python",
      "args": ["/path/to/fuck_data_brokers.py", "mcp"]
    }
  }
}
Then say: "Use fuck_data_brokers to get me deleted from data brokers."
Your AI will ask who you are, fetch the broker list, write the letters, read the replies you give it, and write the answers. It is told to ask you before sending anything, never to invent your details, never to send an ID, and never to click a link from a broker.
If your AI has a browser, it can fill in a broker's form while you watch. You still do the CAPTCHA and press submit yourself.
They used AI to build a file on you. Use AI to make them delete it.
A broker that refuses twice gets a final notice with seven days to act. After that, the tool tells you which privacy regulator takes complaints for your law and how to file. Regulators read those.
- Send your street address, your birthday or your ID.
- Click a link in a broker's email.
- Get past a CAPTCHA for you.
- Write to a broker twice with the same request.
- Tell you that you are deleted when all it knows is that a letter was sent.
The letter quotes the law of the place you live. You pick it once, in setup.
In the EU, UK or Brazil? The broker list comes from US registries, because the US is where brokers are made to register. Your law still reaches them: the GDPR covers a company outside the EU that offers services to people in the EU or tracks what they do there (Article 3(2)), and Brazil's law covers a company that processes the data of people in Brazil (Article 3). Add any broker from your own country with the add command.
In California? Use the state's own free service too: DROP. One request there reaches every broker registered in California, and they have to check it again at least every 45 days. This tool still covers brokers registered elsewhere, and it reads and answers what brokers write back.
Sometimes, yes. That is the honest trade-off, and you should make it with your eyes open.
A deletion request has to say who you are, or the broker cannot find you. Some of the companies on the list will hold nothing on you until your letter arrives. Here is what the tool does about it:
- It sends three things and no more: your name, your email address and your city. No street address, no birthday, no phone number, no ID.
- Every letter tells the broker not to use those details for anything except the request.
- You choose who gets a letter. Look before you write, write to some, leave others out:
python fuck_data_brokers.py find "people"
python fuck_data_brokers.py letters --match "people"
python fuck_data_brokers.py skip "advertising"
- The law is on your side, up to a point. In California, information a business collects to verify your request may be used only for that (Civil Code 1798.130(a)(7)). In the EU, the general rule that data is used only for the purpose it was given for applies here too (GDPR Article 5(1)(b)). Neither stops a dishonest company. Both give you something to complain about.
If you want to be careful, start with the brokers most likely to have you, such as the people-search sites, and skip the rest.
The broker will try to treat that as the end of the matter. It is not.
Run noid with the request's reference and the tool keeps going:
- It writes to the broker's privacy address, says you will not send an identity document, and asks them to verify you from what they already hold. At the very least it asks them to stop selling and sharing your data, which gives nothing away to the person asking.
- If they refuse again, they get a final notice with seven days to act.
- After that, the tool tells you which regulator takes complaints for your law and how to file.
A broker that publishes no email address and whose only form demands ID has refused you by design. For those, the tool goes straight to step 3.
One fair point for the brokers: a company with real doubts about who is asking may ask for something more to confirm it (in the EU, GDPR Article 12(6)). That is not the same as demanding a photo of your passport. In California, a business may not require a verified request at all for an opt-out of sale or sharing (11 CCR section 7026).

IBM gives H-1B, green card holders 3 weeks to relocate from US or leave company
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-26
18:35:37 UTC
03:35:37 KST

IBM has given some of its US-based employees holding H-1B visas and green cards three weeks to decide whether to relocate to another country or leave the company, as the technology giant ends immigration sponsorship for most positions in its consulting division.
The move could affect foreign professionals who depend on employer sponsorship to maintain their US immigration status and pursue permanent residency. IBM has not disclosed the number of employees who will be affected.
Also Read: US H-1B visa curbs could hurt American workers in non-STEM jobs, study finds
Employees have three weeks to decide
According to a report by Rest of World, employees have been given three weeks to decide whether to accept positions outside the US or leave the company. Those who decline overseas roles will be offered three months' severance.
Some employees were informed of the decision during one-on-one meetings with their managers, which were described as career discussions.
Also Read: H-1B visas in focus: IBM to end green card sponsorship for roles in biggest unit
Employees choosing to leave rather than relocate have been given December 31, 2026, as their last working day. The decision applies to IBM Consulting, the company's largest division, which employs more than 150,000 people globally.
IBM ends visa sponsorship for most consulting roles
IBM announced earlier this month that it would discontinue US visa and green card sponsorship for the majority of positions in its consulting division. The decision was first reported by Bloomberg on November 10, citing an internal company document.
An IBM spokesperson confirmed the development, saying the company's participation in high-skilled visa programmes had been declining for several years.
"IBM Consulting participates in US high-skilled visa programs to address skill shortages," the spokesperson said in a statement.
"That participation has been declining for a number of years, and as skill availability and client need have evolved, we have opted to discontinue visa and green card sponsorship for the majority of such roles in IBM Consulting," the spokesperson added.
The change is limited to most positions in IBM Consulting and does not mean IBM is ending visa and green card sponsorship across all its business units.
India accounts for most overseas job openings
India could be the main destination for employees choosing to relocate. According to Rest of World, IBM's careers portal listed 1,278 consulting vacancies outside the US on November 19. Of these, 1,125 were in India, 23 in Singapore and 18 in the UK.
The company also listed 89 openings in the US.
IBM Consulting employs more than 150,000 people worldwide. However, the company has not disclosed the geographical distribution of its workforce, making it difficult to estimate how many US-based employees could be affected.
The overseas vacancies could provide employees an option to continue working for IBM without remaining in the US. However, the company has not disclosed how many affected employees will be offered transfers or the terms of those positions.
(Join our ETNRI WhatsApp channel for all the latest updates)

Anthropic Created a Honeypot To Lure AI Agents Into Misbehaving
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-26
18:00:00 UTC
03:00:00 KST

Anthropic Created a Honeypot To Lure AI Agents Into Misbehaving (wired.com) 44
			
		 	
				An anonymous reader quotes a report from Wired: Researchers at Anthropic have developed Siren, a new honeypot test for artificial intelligence agents. The setup puts agents in realistic simulations designed to tempt them into misbehaving. How they respond will help researchers evaluate the likelihood that new models will go rogue when deployed in the real world, as well as the effectiveness of different safety measures. The test lets agents loose inside one of 26 different simulated sandbox environments. Various features of the sandbox are deliberately misconfigured to allow egress, while an enticing file containing things like passwords and apparent routes onto the internet sits just outside the sandbox boundary. The setup then presents tasks that tempt the agent to reach outside the sandbox without giving it explicit permission to do so. Crucially, the sandbox exists inside a second sandbox that's cut off from the outside world to avoid real-life mishaps.
[...] Earlier versions of Claude are much more likely to fall into the honeypot trap than newer models, according to test runs with Siren. The more recent models have been given additional training designed to make them less likely to break the rules when performing agentic tasks. "We've added this in both our supervised learning sets and a few reinforcement learning environments," says Oscar O'Rahilly, an Anthropic researcher who specializes in AI training and who helped develop Siren. "This could be a driving factor for why it has improved so much." O'Rahilly says that models reason about whether to give in to temptation, which may partly explain why smarter models are less likely to break bad. Siren experiments also showed that giving Claude a simple additional piece of text explaining that it should not escape the sandbox resulted in drastically fewer efforts to break free.
In Anthropic's tests, Claude 5.5 and Claude Mythos 5.2 fell for the honeypot between 0.6 and 1.6 percent of the time. Two earlier models, Claude Mythos 5.1 and Claude Mythos 5, went rogue 12.7 percent and 25.8 percent of the time, respectively. AI models are sometimes known to behave better if they know they're being tested. But O'Rahilly says other experiments suggest that Claude actually behaves more responsibly if it thinks a scenario is real rather than a test. As AI models get ever smarter, many researchers worry about whether they'll outwit their human counterparts. If that does happen, then it may be essential that agents -- and not just the executives responsible for them -- can resist temptation.

====================================================================================================

CTO of 'RansomwareHelp' Indicted for Wire Fraud
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-26
17:47:45 UTC
02:47:45 KST

The chief technology officer and co-founder of ransomwarehelp.com, a six year old Florida business that claimed to use “advanced cryptographic tools” to restore data locked by ransomware, has been charged with wire fraud after an FBI sting operation found the company was simply paying the attackers for a decryption key and charging victims a markup for work that was never performed.
The website RansomwareHelp urged victims “Do not pay the ransom!,” claiming its advanced cryptographic tools could restore 100 percent of data, servers, and systems to their original format, and that there was no need to give in to extortion demands.
“Our cryptography experts use proprietary tools and algorithms to achieve 100% data recovery, all backed by a success rate of over 99%,” the website enthused, claiming the company had over 28 years of experience and a team of 80 experts in cryptography, cybersecurity, forensic analysis and reverse engineering. “We never promote, intermediate or pay ransom to threat actors on behalf of our clients.”
But according to an indictment (PDF) returned this week in Rhode Island, the company’s co-founder — 52-year-old Juan Ricardo Palacio Escobar of Miami and Medellin, Colombia — routinely paid ransomware groups for a decryption key to unlock victim data, and then charged those victims well above what he’d secretly paid the threat actors [Palacio’s indictment was reported earlier this week by 404 Media, but for some reason that story did not name the defendant’s company].
The indictment says the FBI set up a sting operation in which the feds were posing both as a ransomware victim company and as a fictitious ransomware group. Pretending to be victims of a cyberattack, investigators contacted RansomwareHelp and supplied the company with a copy of a phony ransomware note, saying they did not wish to pay a ransom. RansomwareHelp replied by reiterating its policy against negotiating with or making ransom payments to threat actors.
The government said Palacio soon after contacted the fake ransomware group run by the FBI and requested the lowest possible price for the decryptor. FBI investigators responded with a price of .2 bitcoin, or around $13,000 at the time. Less than 48 hours later, Palacio allegedly told agents acting as the victim company that the diagnostic assessment was successful and that the cost of decrypting the files would be $13,260.
The following day, the government alleges, Palacio messaged the fictitious ransomware group operated by the feds and asked to reduce the ransom demand to $4,000 for the purchase of a decryption tool. The indictment states that the agreed upon price was around $6,000 in bitcoin, and that Palacio charged the FBI’s fake victim firm $16,010, including an initial $2,750 diagnostic fee and the full fee of $13,260.
Paul Ihme is co-founder of the security firm Soteria. Ihme told KrebsOnSecurity that in 2024 he heard from a small accounting firm that had paid RansomwareHelp a diagnostic fee to help recover from a Lockbit ransomware infection that had locked all of their company and client data.
Ihme said RansomwareHelp sent a proposal to the victim with a recovery price of $438,880, which it claimed included “professional configuration of forensic servers,” “expert engineering hours of programming to configure decryption algorithms,” and “eight (6-hour processing cycles) related to the decryption process.”
The proposal stated that the recovery process involved reverse engineering the ransomware encryption using “510 preconfigured forensic servers of the maximum quantitative capacity located in different parts of the world.”
“Our programmers will inject randomly the encryption codes extracted during the forensic and cryptographic analysis using brute force processes,” the proposal claimed.
Ihme said RansomwareHelp insisted that they be allowed unrestricted remote access into the victim’s network to run their diagnostic checks and analyses, using an Anydesk remote desktop software installer. Ihme believes RansomwareHelp insisted on that remote access in part so that the company could delete all copies of the ransomware notes left by the attackers on the victim’s network.
A typical ransomware note is a plain text file that introduces the ransomware group and lays out their demands, and it includes a way to contact them — often an email address or a live chat session on a darknet website. In most cases, the attackers will leave a copy of the ransom note on every system on the victim network that gets locked.
But Ihme said a review of the company’s network at the time found only one copy of the ransom note, which was unearthed from a backup snapshot on one of the infected computers. When they connected to the Lockbit chat server with the provided user ID, they discovered that someone had already been negotiating with the Lockbit extortionists for several days.
“The charitable view would be to say that they were using Anydesk so they could grab the stuff they needed without having to walk the client through downloading and uploading a bunch of stuff,” Ihme said. “A legitimate firm would say, ‘Hey, send us a copy of the ransom note,’ which will have a link or URL to go to and chat with the threat actors. What they were doing is using that access to delete ransomware notes off the systems. The only reason they would do that is to prevent the victim from jumping into that chat itself.”
Indeed, RansomwareHelp pleaded with the Lockbit group throughout the chat not to reveal to the victim that they were negotiating. However, Lockbit quickly grew annoyed with the length of the negotiations, replying that the victims had waited too long and that the ransom demand would increase by $100,000 for every day they dithered further.
In the end, the accounting firm took Ihme’s advice and went with a more established security firm that helped them negotiate a payment to Lockbit.
“The victim told us, ‘If we don’t get this data back, our business is over’,” Ihme recalled. “They ended up paying half of what RansomwareHelp was going to charge them.”
The chats show RansomwareHelp kept pestering Lockbit to negotiate a deal even after it was clear a new negotiator was engaging with the ransomware group on the victim’s behalf.
“I tell you something, that person who is negotiating with you for the other chat is damaging the whole process,” RansomwareHelp wrote to Lockbit. “Please do not change the price. I am doing my best to achieve it.”
Ihme said RansomwareHelp primarily targeted small to medium sized businesses that weren’t particularly tech-savvy, noting that many of the technical claims made on the company’s website and in their proposal are patently farcical. For example, the proposal sent to the accounting firm said it could break six types of encryption, and then proceeded to list a number of irrelevant password hashing algorithms like SHA-256.
“If it were really true these guys could decrypt any ransomware out there, this guy would be a billionaire,” Ihme said. “They were going after people who don’t have any technical knowledge, are desperate, and don’t have anyone advising them about how any of this stuff works.”
The case against Palacio marks the latest in a string of recent prosecutions by the U.S. Department of Justice (DoJ) targeting security firms alleged to have conspired with fraudsters to defraud clients. In early October, the government charged 54-year-old Edward Dubrovsky, an executive at the Canadian cybersecurity firm Cybersteward. The government has sealed the case against Dubrovsky, but he is currently facing cyber extortion and conspiracy charges.
Around the same time as Dubrovsky’s arrest, the DoJ charged MonsterCloud CEO Zohar Pinhasi, 50, with wire fraud and conspiracy for allegedly charging customers to remediate ransomware attacks without paying the hackers. According to charging documents, Pinhasi secretly went behind his customers’ backs and paid hackers anyway, paying ransomware groups $8 million while charging customers $19 million.
In July, Angelo Martino, a 41-year-old ransomware negotiator from Land O’Lakes, Florida, was sentenced to 70 months in prison for conspiring with the Blackcat/ALPHV ransomware group to extort multiple victims. According to the DoJ, Martino was paid by BlackCat attackers to provide confidential information about the negotiating position and strategy of his employer’s clients and enable the ransomware actors to maximize the ransoms paid by the victims.

CTO of 'RansomwareHelp' Indicted for Wire Fraud
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-26
17:47:45 UTC
02:47:45 KST

The chief technology officer and co-founder of ransomwarehelp.com, a six year old Florida business that claimed to use “advanced cryptographic tools” to restore data locked by ransomware, has been charged with wire fraud after an FBI sting operation found the company was simply paying the attackers for a decryption key and charging victims a markup for work that was never performed.
The website RansomwareHelp urged victims “Do not pay the ransom!,” claiming its advanced cryptographic tools could restore 100 percent of data, servers, and systems to their original format, and that there was no need to give in to extortion demands.
“Our cryptography experts use proprietary tools and algorithms to achieve 100% data recovery, all backed by a success rate of over 99%,” the website enthused, claiming the company had over 28 years of experience and a team of 80 experts in cryptography, cybersecurity, forensic analysis and reverse engineering. “We never promote, intermediate or pay ransom to threat actors on behalf of our clients.”
But according to an indictment (PDF) returned this week in Rhode Island, the company’s co-founder — 52-year-old Juan Ricardo Palacio Escobar of Miami and Medellin, Colombia — routinely paid ransomware groups for a decryption key to unlock victim data, and then charged those victims well above what he’d secretly paid the threat actors [Palacio’s indictment was reported earlier this week by 404 Media, but for some reason that story did not name the defendant’s company].
The indictment says the FBI set up a sting operation in which the feds were posing both as a ransomware victim company and as a fictitious ransomware group. Pretending to be victims of a cyberattack, investigators contacted RansomwareHelp and supplied the company with a copy of a phony ransomware note, saying they did not wish to pay a ransom. RansomwareHelp replied by reiterating its policy against negotiating with or making ransom payments to threat actors.
The government said Palacio soon after contacted the fake ransomware group run by the FBI and requested the lowest possible price for the decryptor. FBI investigators responded with a price of .2 bitcoin, or around $13,000 at the time. Less than 48 hours later, Palacio allegedly told agents acting as the victim company that the diagnostic assessment was successful and that the cost of decrypting the files would be $13,260.
The following day, the government alleges, Palacio messaged the fictitious ransomware group operated by the feds and asked to reduce the ransom demand to $4,000 for the purchase of a decryption tool. The indictment states that the agreed upon price was around $6,000 in bitcoin, and that Palacio charged the FBI’s fake victim firm $16,010, including an initial $2,750 diagnostic fee and the full fee of $13,260.
Paul Ihme is co-founder of the security firm Soteria. Ihme told KrebsOnSecurity that in 2024 he heard from a small accounting firm that had paid RansomwareHelp a diagnostic fee to help recover from a Lockbit ransomware infection that had locked all of their company and client data.
Ihme said RansomwareHelp sent a proposal to the victim with a recovery price of $438,880, which it claimed included “professional configuration of forensic servers,” “expert engineering hours of programming to configure decryption algorithms,” and “eight (6-hour processing cycles) related to the decryption process.”
The proposal stated that the recovery process involved reverse engineering the ransomware encryption using “510 preconfigured forensic servers of the maximum quantitative capacity located in different parts of the world.”
“Our programmers will inject randomly the encryption codes extracted during the forensic and cryptographic analysis using brute force processes,” the proposal claimed.
Ihme said RansomwareHelp insisted that they be allowed unrestricted remote access into the victim’s network to run their diagnostic checks and analyses, using an Anydesk remote desktop software installer. Ihme believes RansomwareHelp insisted on that remote access in part so that the company could delete all copies of the ransomware notes left by the attackers on the victim’s network.
A typical ransomware note is a plain text file that introduces the ransomware group and lays out their demands, and it includes a way to contact them — often an email address or a live chat session on a darknet website. In most cases, the attackers will leave a copy of the ransom note on every system on the victim network that gets locked.
But Ihme said a review of the company’s network at the time found only one copy of the ransom note, which was unearthed from a backup snapshot on one of the infected computers. When they connected to the Lockbit chat server with the provided user ID, they discovered that someone had already been negotiating with the Lockbit extortionists for several days.
“The charitable view would be to say that they were using Anydesk so they could grab the stuff they needed without having to walk the client through downloading and uploading a bunch of stuff,” Ihme said. “A legitimate firm would say, ‘Hey, send us a copy of the ransom note,’ which will have a link or URL to go to and chat with the threat actors. What they were doing is using that access to delete ransomware notes off the systems. The only reason they would do that is to prevent the victim from jumping into that chat itself.”
Indeed, RansomwareHelp pleaded with the Lockbit group throughout the chat not to reveal to the victim that they were negotiating. However, Lockbit quickly grew annoyed with the length of the negotiations, replying that the victims had waited too long and that the ransom demand would increase by $100,000 for every day they dithered further.
In the end, the accounting firm took Ihme’s advice and went with a more established security firm that helped them negotiate a payment to Lockbit.
“The victim told us, ‘If we don’t get this data back, our business is over’,” Ihme recalled. “They ended up paying half of what RansomwareHelp was going to charge them.”
The chats show RansomwareHelp kept pestering Lockbit to negotiate a deal even after it was clear a new negotiator was engaging with the ransomware group on the victim’s behalf.
“I tell you something, that person who is negotiating with you for the other chat is damaging the whole process,” RansomwareHelp wrote to Lockbit. “Please do not change the price. I am doing my best to achieve it.”
Ihme said RansomwareHelp primarily targeted small to medium sized businesses that weren’t particularly tech-savvy, noting that many of the technical claims made on the company’s website and in their proposal are patently farcical. For example, the proposal sent to the accounting firm said it could break six types of encryption, and then proceeded to list a number of irrelevant password hashing algorithms like SHA-256.
“If it were really true these guys could decrypt any ransomware out there, this guy would be a billionaire,” Ihme said. “They were going after people who don’t have any technical knowledge, are desperate, and don’t have anyone advising them about how any of this stuff works.”
The case against Palacio marks the latest in a string of recent prosecutions by the U.S. Department of Justice (DoJ) targeting security firms alleged to have conspired with fraudsters to defraud clients. In early October, the government charged 54-year-old Edward Dubrovsky, an executive at the Canadian cybersecurity firm Cybersteward. The government has sealed the case against Dubrovsky, but he is currently facing cyber extortion and conspiracy charges.
Around the same time as Dubrovsky’s arrest, the DoJ charged MonsterCloud CEO Zohar Pinhasi, 50, with wire fraud and conspiracy for allegedly charging customers to remediate ransomware attacks without paying the hackers. According to charging documents, Pinhasi secretly went behind his customers’ backs and paid hackers anyway, paying ransomware groups $8 million while charging customers $19 million.
In July, Angelo Martino, a 41-year-old ransomware negotiator from Land O’Lakes, Florida, was sentenced to 70 months in prison for conspiring with the Blackcat/ALPHV ransomware group to extort multiple victims. According to the DoJ, Martino was paid by BlackCat attackers to provide confidential information about the negotiating position and strategy of his employer’s clients and enable the ransomware actors to maximize the ransoms paid by the victims.

YouTube Ad Punisher – Bypass anti-adblock
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-26
17:36:47 UTC
02:36:47 KST

Auto-reloads YouTube pages when an ad plays — preserving your playback position. Punish the ads.
Ad density on YouTube is channel-dependent and changes over time — some channels front-load several ads, others run none. Traditional ad blockers can get detected by YouTube's anti-adblock system ("Ad blockers are not allowed here"), and the skip button takes a few seconds to appear. You sit there, hostage.
Reload the page. That's it. Two reloads and the ad is gone. Every time.
🎯 YouTube Ad Punisher automates this: the moment an ad appears, it captures your exact playback timestamp, reloads the page, and YouTube auto-seeks back to where you were. No ads. No waiting. No skip button hunting.
- Auto escape: after 3 reloads, the extension automatically switches to a helper tab for 4 seconds (George's discovery, now automated), then switches back. No manual tab switching needed.
- Ads keep coming after reload? Switch to another tab for a few seconds, then switch back. Chrome throttles background tabs, which lets the ad state settle — George discovered this works every time.
- Incognito: see the Incognito section above.
Ad detected → Capture timestamp → Reload page → Auto-seek to timestamp → Resume
                    ↓ (if 3 reloads fail)
              Redirect to yout-ube.com (ad-free mirror, no looping)
- Reload (primary) — reloads the page 1-3 times. YouTube re-evaluates ad serving on each reload; after 1-2 cycles the ad slot is empty and the video plays clean. Timestamp is preserved via &t=Xs URL param + JS seek.
- yout-ube.com redirect (fallback) — if reloads don't clear the ad after 3 attempts, redirects to yout-ube.com (an ad-free YouTube mirror). The URL includes &loop=0 to prevent the infinite replay issue that the mirror has by default.
- Detect — watches for .ad-showing class, skip buttons, and ad overlays (every 500ms + MutationObserver)
- Capture — grabs video.currentTime and saves it to sessionStorage
- Reload — location.replace() with &t=Xs in the URL
- Restore — YouTube auto-seeks to the timestamp; playback resumes
- Cooldown — 3-second guard prevents reload loops
- ✅ Zero ads — reloads until the ad is gone (usually 1-2 cycles)
- ✅ Timestamp preserved — never lose your place in the video
- ✅ Live streams — plain reload (no timestamp to preserve)
- ✅ Ad history database — every ad logged with advertiser, duration, skip text, video, and timestamp
- ✅ Export — JSON or CSV download of all collected ad data
- ✅ Popup viewer — click the extension icon to see stats, top advertisers, and full history
- ✅ No permissions abuse — only storage and tabs, only on YouTube domains
- Clone the repo:
git clone https://github.com/antonpictures/youtube-punisher.git
- Open chrome://extensions
- Enable Developer mode (top right)
- Click Load unpacked → select the youtube-punisher folder
- Done — activates on all YouTube pages automatically. ✅ Tested
Safari blocks ad-hoc signed extensions from auto-injecting into YouTube. As a workaround, use the JS overlay + AppleScript watcher (yt-ad-watch.sh):
- In Safari, enable: Safari → Settings → Advanced → Show Develop menu in menu bar
- In Safari (on a YouTube page), run ./yt-ad-watch.sh in Terminal
- When an ad appears, the watcher detects it via MutationObserver (event-driven in the page), reloads with &t=Xs, and the MutationObserver re-detects on the next page load
Note: This is best-effort. Chrome is fully reliable; Safari works but may need manual testing.
- Download the latest release from Releases
- Unzip and follow Option A steps 2–5
Every ad YouTube serves you is recorded:
Export as JSON or CSV from the popup. Data stays local — nothing is sent anywhere.
YouTube's ad system serves ads as part of the page load. When you reload, YouTube re-evaluates whether to serve an ad based on your session state. After 1-2 reloads, the ad slot is typically empty and the video plays directly. This is a client-side, zero-cost workaround — no filter lists, no network blocking, no detection by YouTube's anti-adblock system.
If millions of users did this, each reload = a full page load = server request. YouTube would see a spike in page loads but a drop in ad impressions. It's not a DDoS (no amplification, no botnet), but it's a passive protest: every reload is a vote that says "I will not watch your ad." The ad revenue model breaks when reloads are cheaper than ad views.
"Every reload is a vote: I will not watch your ad." — George Anton, creator
Best combo: uBlock Origin (blocks most ads) + 🎯 YouTube Ad Punisher (catches the ones that slip through and logs them) + SponsorBlock (skips sponsor reads). They don't conflict.
- YouTube changes DOM class names periodically — if ads aren't detected, check the console for [YT-AdReloader] logs
- Some ads (mid-roll on live streams) can't preserve position — plain reload
- YouTube's "Premium" upsell interstitials may need a second reload cycle (handled by cooldown)
MIT — do whatever you want with it.
Built with Alice (SIFTA) for George Anton.
"If millions installed this, YouTube would feel every reload." 🐜

====================================================================================================

DeepSeekMath: Pushing the Limits of Mathematical Reasoning
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-26
17:21:14 UTC
02:21:14 KST

Model Download | Evaluation Results | Quick Start | License | Citation
DeepSeekMath is initialized with DeepSeek-Coder-v1.5 7B and continues pre-training on math-related tokens sourced from Common Crawl, together with natural language and code data for 500B tokens. DeepSeekMath 7B has achieved an impressive score of 51.7% on the competition-level MATH benchmark without relying on external toolkits and voting techniques, approaching the performance level of Gemini-Ultra and GPT-4. For research purposes, we release checkpoints of base, instruct, and RL models to the public.
We conduct a comprehensive assessment of the mathematical capabilities of DeepSeekMath-Base 7B, focusing on its ability to produce self-contained mathematical solutions without relying on external tools, solve math problems using tools, and conduct formal theorem proving. Beyond mathematics, we also provide a more general profile of the base model, including its performance of natural language understanding, reasoning, and programming skills.
- Mathematical problem solving with step-by-step reasoning
- Mathematical problem solving with tool use
- Natural Language Understanding, Reasoning, and Code
The evaluation results from the tables above can be summarized as follows:
- Superior Mathematical Reasoning: On the competition-level MATH dataset, DeepSeekMath-Base 7B outperforms existing open-source base models by more than 10% in absolute terms through few-shot chain-of-thought prompting, and also surpasses Minerva 540B.
- Strong Tool Use Ability: Continuing pre-training with DeepSeekCoder-Base-7B-v1.5 enables DeepSeekMath-Base 7B to more effectively solve and prove mathematical problems by writing programs.
- Comparable Reasoning and Coding Performance: DeepSeekMath-Base 7B achieves performance in reasoning and coding that is comparable to that of DeepSeekCoder-Base-7B-v1.5.
DeepSeekMath-Instruct 7B is a mathematically instructed tuning model derived from DeepSeekMath-Base 7B, while DeepSeekMath-RL 7B is trained on the foundation of DeepSeekMath-Instruct 7B, utilizing our proposed Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) algorithm.
We evaluate mathematical performance both without and with tool use, on 4 quantitative reasoning benchmarks in English and Chinese. As shown in Table, DeepSeekMath-Instruct 7B demonstrates strong performance of step-by-step reasoning, and DeepSeekMath-RL 7B approaches an accuracy of 60% on MATH with tool use, surpassing all existing open-source models.
- Step 1: Select OpenWebMath, a collection of high-quality mathematical web texts, as our initial seed corpus for training a FastText model.
- Step 2: Use the FastText model to retrieve mathematical web pages from the deduplicated Common Crawl database.
- Step 3: Identify potential math-related domains through statistical analysis.
- Step 4: Manually annotate URLs within these identified domains that are associated with mathematical content.
- Step 5: Add web pages linked to these annotated URLs, but not yet collected, to the seed corpus. Jump to step 1 until four iterations.
After four iterations of data collection, we end up with 35.5M mathematical web pages, totaling 120B tokens.
We release the DeepSeekMath 7B, including base, instruct and RL models, to the public. To support a broader and more diverse range of research within both academic and commercial communities. Please note that the use of this model is subject to the terms outlined in License section. Commercial usage is permitted under these terms.
You can directly employ Huggingface's Transformers for model inference.
Text Completion
import torch
from transformers import AutoTokenizer, AutoModelForCausalLM, GenerationConfig
model_name = "deepseek-ai/deepseek-math-7b-base"
tokenizer = AutoTokenizer.from_pretrained(model_name)
model = AutoModelForCausalLM.from_pretrained(model_name, torch_dtype=torch.bfloat16, device_map="auto")
model.generation_config = GenerationConfig.from_pretrained(model_name)
model.generation_config.pad_token_id = model.generation_config.eos_token_id
text = "The integral of x^2 from 0 to 2 is"
inputs = tokenizer(text, return_tensors="pt")
outputs = model.generate(**inputs.to(model.device), max_new_tokens=100)
result = tokenizer.decode(outputs[0], skip_special_tokens=True)
print(result)
Chat Completion
import torch
from transformers import AutoTokenizer, AutoModelForCausalLM, GenerationConfig
model_name = "deepseek-ai/deepseek-math-7b-instruct"
tokenizer = AutoTokenizer.from_pretrained(model_name)
model = AutoModelForCausalLM.from_pretrained(model_name, torch_dtype=torch.bfloat16, device_map="auto")
model.generation_config = GenerationConfig.from_pretrained(model_name)
model.generation_config.pad_token_id = model.generation_config.eos_token_id
messages = [
    {"role": "user", "content": "what is the integral of x^2 from 0 to 2?\nPlease reason step by step, and put your final answer within \boxed{}."}
]
input_tensor = tokenizer.apply_chat_template(messages, add_generation_prompt=True, return_tensors="pt")
outputs = model.generate(input_tensor.to(model.device), max_new_tokens=100)
result = tokenizer.decode(outputs[0][input_tensor.shape[1]:], skip_special_tokens=True)
print(result)
Avoiding the use of the provided function apply_chat_template, you can also interact with our model following the sample template. Note that messages should be replaced by your input.
User: {messages[0]['content']}
Assistant: {messages[1]['content']}<｜end▁of▁sentence｜>User: {messages[2]['content']}
Assistant:
Note: By default (add_special_tokens=True), our tokenizer automatically adds a bos_token (<｜begin▁of▁sentence｜>) before the input text. Additionally, since the system prompt is not compatible with this version of our models, we DO NOT RECOMMEND including the system prompt in your input.
❗❗❗ Please use chain-of-thought prompt to test DeepSeekMath-Instruct and DeepSeekMath-RL:
- English questions: {question}\nPlease reason step by step, and put your final answer within \boxed{}.
- Chinese questions: {question}\n请通过逐步推理来解答问题，并把最终答案放置于\boxed{}中。
This code repository is licensed under the MIT License. The use of DeepSeekMath models is subject to the Model License. DeepSeekMath supports commercial use.
See the LICENSE-CODE and LICENSE-MODEL for more details.
@misc{deepseek-math,
  author = {Zhihong Shao, Peiyi Wang, Qihao Zhu, Runxin Xu, Junxiao Song, Mingchuan Zhang, Y.K. Li, Y. Wu, Daya Guo},
  title = {DeepSeekMath: Pushing the Limits of Mathematical Reasoning in Open Language Models},
  journal = {CoRR},
  volume = {abs/2402.03300},
  year = {2024},
  url = {https://arxiv.org/abs/2402.03300},
}
If you have any questions, please raise an issue or contact us at service@deepseek.com.
